      "title": "11 of the best restaurants in Krakow",
      "content": "In a restored Renaissance palace, the boutique, five-star Pod Roza is Krakow’s oldest hotel, said to have hosted everyone from Franz Liszt to Tsar Alexander I. A splendid setting then for its eponymous restaurant, whose produce-led, modern take on Polish food traditions is no afterthought. Go à la carte or upgrade to a six or eight-course tasting menu — with the option of a well-priced set lunch menu, if a packed afternoon awaits you. Dishes include the likes of wild mushroom velouté with sour cream and meats such as the prized Iberian prey, served with edamame. There’s also a creative, fine-dining spin on the country’s beloved pierogi dumplings, as well as vodka snacks that are a mainstay of Polish weddings. It’s worth booking a room, so you can not only hop into bed happy but also wake [...] piekarnia-zaczyn.pl\n\n## 8. Bufet, Kazimierz\n\nBufet is one of the best restaurants in Krakow\n\n££ | BOOK AHEAD | Best for exciting modern Polish\n\nThe brainchild of Przemyslaw Klima from Bottiglieria 1881, Bufet is one of the most exciting food concepts in Krakow. It artfully blends Polish comfort food with international influences under a fine-dining framework. Traditional ingredients such as oscypek — a smoked ewe’s milk cheese made in the Polish mountains — local trout and blood sausage are jazzed up with chimichurri, yuzu and jalapeños. The menu changes seasonally, but one mainstay is karpatka — a classic cream cake named after the Carpathian Mountains — made nostalgically to mother’s recipe and served with fruit.\n\nbufetkrk.com\n\n## 9. Kluska na Placu, old town [...] Poland’s first restaurant to be awarded two Michelin stars, Bottiglieria 1881 offers one of the country’s finest gastronomic experiences. The 16-course tasting menu is a masterclass in Polish produce, taking diners on a delectable journey into the childhood memories of the chef-owner Przemyslaw Klima. Seasonal dishes, drawing heavily on the larder of the surrounding Lesser Poland region, bring ingredients such as delicate white asparagus, strawberries, sour cream, chamomile, larch and lovage to the fore. The wine selection is impressive, featuring increasingly popular bottles from central Europe and Poland, some of which haven’t had to travel far from the chalky limestone soils around Krakow itself.\n\n1881.com.pl\n\n## 2. Pod Roza, old town\n\nPod Roza is one of the best restaurants in Krakow",
      "score": 0.35952207,
      "raw_content": "## Accessibility Links\n\n![The Times & The Sunday Times](https://www.thetimes.com/wp-content/plugins/tm-wp-frontend/assets/images/the-times-logo.webp)\n\n# 11 of the best restaurants in Krakow\n\n## Take a tour of this southern Polish city’s idiosyncratic culinary scene, and try everything from humble pierogi dumplings to inventive fine dining — set against a historic backdrop\n\n![Kluska na Placu is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/6608ab1d-6d31-449a-a152-5fbb7fabb069.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&crop=2880px%2C1620px%2C0px%2C0px&resize=2360 \"NPHERO 02_Kluska na Placu_012\")\n\n### Share this article\n\nLong considered the cradle of Polish language and literature, and a home to famous writers — not to mention a former capital — Krakow is steeped in history and tradition. Its cobbled streets, medieval squares and magnificent buildings survived the Second World War remarkably intact; today they form a fascinating backdrop to any foodie forays. Influenced by Jewish, Tatar, Armenian, Ukrainian and Roma communities, the culinary offerings here can be tasted amid the hidden cellars of centuries-old townhouses, in chic, contemporary dining rooms and on sunny decks by the Vistula River.\n\nI’ve been a frequent visitor to Krakow for years, first arriving from Warsaw by train, later hopping on a low-cost flight from London with every chance I could get. While deeply rooted in Poland’s past, local chefs are open to new ideas and aren’t afraid to experiment either — just like the city itself. Here are the best restaurants in Krakow to discover its idiosyncratic cuisine.\n\n## Most commented\n\n### [Oxford Street pedestrianisation chaos as cyclists carry on regardless](https://www.thetimes.com/uk/london/article/oxford-street-pedestrianisation-sadiq-khan-cyclists-rw5vlg00x)\n\n![Two police officers speak to a cyclist on the pedestrianised section of Oxford Street.](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/a57283d0-961c-418a-be6f-c31f73f1d2b1.jpg?w=2411)\n\n### [Howe and Nagelsmann have backing at Man United if Carrick is axed](https://www.thetimes.com/sport/football/article/michael-carrick-man-united-future-latest-omar-berrada-jason-wilcox-h3s0p05rs)\n\n![Michael Carrick arriving at Carrington in a black Land Rover Defender.](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/a1b804be-a171-4059-a527-7c2a3a8b8900.jpg?w=1540)\n\n### [Shabana Mahmood announces inquiry into Henry Nowak murder](https://www.thetimes.com/uk/politics/article/henry-nowak-murder-case-home-secretary-review-djg6jllsj)\n\n![Handout photo of Henry Nowak, 18.](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/72302c8a-1685-41f8-b6f0-15ccc0c1d5ac.jpg?w=1482)\n\n### [Meta launches bn share sale to bankroll AI expansion](https://www.thetimes.com/us/business-us/article/meta-fundraising-95-billion-stock-bankroll-ai-expansion-qsws8ljgr)\n\n![Meta's CEO Mark Zuckerberg mimics virtual reality glasses during a live recording panel at a technology podcast.](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/391f9d98-d86e-43ee-997d-b8f9150fedb6.jpg?w=4711)\n\n### [Macron offers colleges €5bn to appease student protesters](https://www.thetimes.com/world/europe/article/macron-offers-colleges-5bn-to-appease-student-protesters-3jqvj7c9k)\n\n![Protesters dance around a fire in a public square during demonstrations over the cost of living in Marseille.](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/4e192e6d-09ae-43eb-a84f-383afd37affb.jpg?w=7728)\n\n*This article contains affiliate links that will earn us revenue*\n\n## 1. Bottiglieria 1881, Kazimierz\n\n![Bottiglieria 1881 is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/18a4590a-b874-44b3-8fa8-29f5b8fde1e8.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&resize=1328)\n\n£££ | BOOK AHEAD | **Best for** creative fine dining\n\n## Top stories\n\n### [How Meghan has struggled in private since her very public marriage](https://www.thetimes.com/life-style/celebrity/article/has-meghan-struggled-in-private-since-her-very-public-marriage-20q9bkvf9)\n\n### [At 39, I was married to a good man. So why did I end things?](https://www.thetimes.com/life-style/sex-relationships/article/why-i-left-my-marriage-rqjn7glv9)\n\nPoland’s first restaurant to be awarded two Michelin stars, Bottiglieria 1881 offers one of the country’s finest gastronomic experiences. The 16-course tasting menu is a masterclass in Polish produce, taking diners on a delectable journey into the childhood memories of the chef-owner Przemyslaw Klima. Seasonal dishes, drawing heavily on the larder of the surrounding Lesser Poland region, bring ingredients such as delicate white asparagus, strawberries, sour cream, chamomile, larch and lovage to the fore. The wine selection is impressive, featuring increasingly popular bottles from central Europe and Poland, some of which haven’t had to travel far from the chalky limestone soils around [Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/travel/destinations/europe-travel/poland/krakow) itself.\n\n*1881.com.pl*\n\n## 2. Pod Roza, old town\n\n![Pod Roza is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/70c534f3-fe12-4644-9cac-16e4a695b29c.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&resize=1328)\n\n£££ | BAR | **Best for** a date night in the historic city centre\n\n## Top stories\n\n### [Oxford Street pedestrianisation chaos as cyclists carry on regardless](https://www.thetimes.com/uk/london/article/oxford-street-pedestrianisation-sadiq-khan-cyclists-rw5vlg00x)\n\n### [Curator of ‘Scotland’s most exotic garden’ killed during Storm Boelo](https://www.thetimes.com/uk/scotland/article/storm-buelo-richard-baines-logan-garden-death-dm7xh5dqh)\n\n### [Howe and Nagelsmann have backing at Man United if Carrick is axed](https://www.thetimes.com/sport/football/article/michael-carrick-man-united-future-latest-omar-berrada-jason-wilcox-h3s0p05rs)\n\n### [Macron offers colleges €5bn to appease student protesters](https://www.thetimes.com/world/europe/article/macron-offers-colleges-5bn-to-appease-student-protesters-3jqvj7c9k)\n\n### [Meta launches bn share sale to bankroll AI expansion](https://www.thetimes.com/us/business-us/article/meta-fundraising-95-billion-stock-bankroll-ai-expansion-qsws8ljgr)\n\nIn a restored Renaissance palace, the boutique, five-star Pod Roza is Krakow’s oldest hotel, said to have hosted everyone from Franz Liszt to Tsar Alexander I. A splendid setting then for its eponymous restaurant, whose produce-led, modern take on Polish food traditions is no afterthought. Go à la carte or upgrade to a six or eight-course tasting menu — with the option of a well-priced set lunch menu, if a packed afternoon awaits you. Dishes include the likes of wild mushroom velouté with sour cream and meats such as the prized Iberian prey, served with edamame. There’s also a creative, fine-dining spin on the country’s beloved pierogi dumplings, as well as vodka snacks that are a mainstay of Polish weddings. It’s worth booking a room, so you can not only hop into bed happy but also wake up to the hotel’s spa and wellness centre, in a medieval cellar.\n\n## 3. Smakolyki, Piasek\n\n![Smakolyki is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/5b795fe9-2fff-4cca-b957-1338f39bbad9.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&resize=2360)\n\n£ | **Best for** affordable Polish classics\n\nA two-minute walk from the main campus of Jagiellonian University — one of the oldest in the world — Smakolyki has been bringing solace to homesick students since 2011. Traditional, affordable and authentic, the food transports you into the warm embrace of a Polish home. Filled with beloved classics, the menu spans the likes of fermented beetroot soup and crispy potato pancakes, as well as various types of pierogi such as quark and potato. Lard-fried schabowy is a breaded pork schnitzel that, together with dill-sprinkled potatoes and sauerkraut, constitutes a traditional Sunday lunch. In an airy space within a historic building, Smakolyki has tall, arched windows that overlook the leafy expanse of Planty, one of the city’s largest parks.\n\n*smakolyki.eu*\n\n## 4. Kaplony i Szczezuje, Kazimierz\n\n![Kaplony i Szczezuje is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/317e8b46-892e-4171-ab60-38a1c33d6d47.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&resize=2360)\n\n££ | BOOK AHEAD | **Best for** heritage flavours\n\nKrakow’s historic Jewish district, Kazimierz, is home to restaurants set along cobbled streets delivering a traditional taste of Jewish fare. One of the best is Kaplony I Szczezuje, run by renowned chefs Agnieszka Kraszewska and Aleksander Baron. Here, the pair are on a mission to revive the cuisine of Cracovian Jews; inspired by recipes found in long-forgotten cookery books, their modus operandi is to recreate pre-war dishes for the modern punter. For instance, try their “Jewish-style caviar”: boletus mushroom and aubergine, served with hazelnuts and fermented cucumber on toasted challah bread. Or you could opt for cabbage rolls with roasted goat, and the likes of veal tongue in an anchovy sauce. The menu changes seasonally and there’s a considered list of craft vodkas and Polish gins.\n\n*kaplonyiszczezuje.pl*\n\n## 5. Molam Thai Canteen & Bar, Piasek\n\n![Molam Thai Canteen & Bar is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/d8e4e2c1-38a1-4f58-80cd-e5aaaad15b01.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&resize=2360)\n\n£ | BOOK AHEAD | **Best for** modern Thai\n\nNot many Asian restaurants in [Poland](https://www.thetimes.com/travel/destinations/europe-travel/poland) have a Bib Gourmand. Molam was inspired by Bangkok’s late-night canteens and the regional flavours of Thailand. And the founder and chef Tomek Muza even shook the pans at the Smoking Goat in London’s Soho before moving back to Poland for his modern Thai venture. The menu is filled with little-known regional specialities from various corners of the country — like khao soi gai, a zesty northern Thai noodle soup with chicken, pepped up with pickled mustard greens and chilli jam. Each dish explodes with flavours from pickled papaya to fragrant Thai herbs and charred chillies. The open kitchen with counter seating adds to the fun atmosphere, and the eclectic playlist includes modern “molam” — a style rooted in rural Thai folk music.\n\n*molam.pl*\n\n## 6. Nat Bistro, Kazimierz\n\n![Nat Bistro is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/9cd88093-1192-46d9-ab26-efdd273522d4.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&resize=1328)\n\n£ | BOOK AHEAD | **Best for** natural wines\n\nIn a quarter of Krakow known for its vibrant nightlife and vivid array of trendy bars and restaurants, Nat Bistro fits the bill perfectly. This casual spot near a medieval square has whitewashed brick walls dotted with the work of local artists. Friendly, knowledgeable staff will walk you through an impressive collection of natural wines from artisanal vineyards scattered across Europe. They pair perfectly with the rolling menu of bistro-style sharing plates: think artisanal Polish cheeses, creative takes on seasonal vegetables and inventive main courses such as charred squid with mangalitsa blood sausage and celeriac.\n\n*instagram.com/nat.bistro*\n\nYou’ve successfully signed up to the **Travel newsletter**\n\n[Explore our newsletters](https://home.thetimes.com/myNews)\n\nAn error occurred. Please use the link below. **Travel newsletter**\n\n[Explore our newsletters](https://home.thetimes.com/myNews)\n\n## 7. Zaczyn, Zwierzyniec\n\n![Zaczyn is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/02913fe5-d08c-475c-b427-e6eb1a69916a.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&resize=2360)\n\n£ | **Best for** lazy, plant-based brunches\n\nCelebrated in local folk culture, bread has always been one of the most crucial components of Poland’s culinary heritage. The post-communist transition of the early Nineties saw a shift in crunchy loaves from family-owned bakeries to supermarkets and convenience stores — but over the last decade, artisanal bakeries have been on the rise. Zofia Barto started Zaczyn as a small, plant-based bakery; today, she runs a popular breakfast and brunch spot in a restored tenement house near the Vistula River. Zaczyn’s shelves are filled with shapely rye loaves, shining babkas and puffy challah, and the menu features a tempting, ever-changing array along the lines of einkorn brioche sandwiches (from the wild heritage grain), whole grain congees and sourdough toast with homemade plant-based ricotta.\n\n*piekarnia-zaczyn.pl*\n\n## 8. Bufet, Kazimierz\n\n![Bufet is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/f990982c-d959-412b-87e1-6a9ea2b4fe8e.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&resize=2360)\n\n££ | BOOK AHEAD | **Best for** exciting modern Polish\n\nThe brainchild of Przemyslaw Klima from Bottiglieria 1881, Bufet is one of the most exciting food concepts in Krakow. It artfully blends Polish comfort food with international influences under a fine-dining framework. Traditional ingredients such as oscypek — a smoked ewe’s milk cheese made in the Polish mountains — local trout and blood sausage are jazzed up with chimichurri, yuzu and jalapeños. The menu changes seasonally, but one mainstay is karpatka — a classic cream cake named after the Carpathian Mountains — made nostalgically to mother’s recipe and served with fruit.\n\n*bufetkrk.com*\n\n## 9. Kluska na Placu, old town\n\n![Kluska na Placu is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/9d16f071-8b52-4961-a2a2-7a86ee1b0888.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&resize=2360)\n\n££ | **Best for** fabulous family feasts\n\nIn one of the neoclassical townhouses that flank Szczepanski Square, Kluska na Placu has an exciting menu that appeals to all generations. Blending the best of Polish food with flavourful inflections from the transnational region of Silesia (spanning part of southwest Poland), Kluska na Placu serves updated spins on the classics. Nostalgic, retro-chic interiors allude to the communist era and the menu has a section dedicated to Silesia’s renowned dumplings — shapely potato balls coated in delicious sauces. Go meaty with the likes of beef and gherkin stew, or try more contemporary versions, like a vegan sauce of braised oyster mushrooms and pickled red onion. It’s a fun introduction to the flavours of Poland.\n\n*kluskanaplacu.pl*\n\n## 10. Kropka, Kazimierz\n\n![Kropka is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/0da08959-6015-4c05-b5ce-915ae0d5a8ca.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&resize=2360)\n\n££ | **Best for** refined sharing plates and boutique bins\n\nPerfect for an evening with friends or a fun date night, this cool, casual spot in Kazimierz buzzes with young Krakow foodies clinking glasses of skin-contact wines from boutique vineyards. Kropka specialises in sharing plates and natural wines, and its ever-changing menu fuses seasonal produce and local culinary traditions with influences and ingredients from all over the world. With the chef Szymon Sierant at the helm, Kropka has become famous city-wide for its bold, multi-layered dishes, such as dehydrated beetroot with sour cream, horseradish and radicchio, or ham hock pithivier with hoisin sauce and kimchi.\n\n*instagram.com/kropka\\_krakow*\n\n## 11. Karakter, Kazimierz\n\n![Karakter is one of the best restaurants in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/99df36f7-6525-4a77-a260-22ffdb1f51bc.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&resize=2360)\n\n££ | BOOK AHEAD | **Best for** nose-to-tail cooking\n\nOn a leafy street near one of Poland’s oldest synagogues, Karakter is well-loved for its lively yet laid-back atmosphere. The menu revolves around meat, and Karakter prides itself on turning less popular cuts into palate-pleasing wonders. Such an approach draws on the long-standing tradition of cooking with offal in both Polish and Jewish culinary culture. At Karakter, pig’s ears are fried into a savoury, crispy tempura, dumplings encase a tender filling of pork brawn, and goose gizzards — a traditional speciality of Polish Jews — are simmered with ginger and orange into a zesty goulash. Rest easy though, there are plenty of options for slightly less adventurous eaters, as well as a great selection of natural wines, artisanal beers and craft cocktails.\n\n*instagram.com/karakter\\_\\_\\_restaurant*\n\n**[Best things to do in Krakow](https://www.thetimes.com/travel/destinations/europe-travel/poland/krakow/best-things-to-do-in-krakow-0c0rj2g0w)**  \n**[Best affordable city breaks in Europe](https://www.thetimes.com/travel/destinations/europe-travel/best-budget-city-breaks-in-europe-njwdwb8hq)**\n\n*Do you have a favourite spot to eat in Krakow? Share your tips in the comments* *below*\n\n### Share this article\n\n### Related articles\n\n![11 of the best restaurants in Edinburgh](https://cms.thetimes.com/wp-content/uploads/image-server/production/1bd54b54-322c-4348-9516-8cb91768357e.jpg?amp;sourceHeight=900&strip=all&format=webp&crop=0px%2C0px%2C1600px%2C900px&w=1328&resize=750)\n\n### 11 of the best restaurants in Edinburgh\n\n![I’ve lived on the French Riviera for years, these are my top restaurants in Nice](https://www.thetimes.com/imageserver/image/498c79e0-14ce-4cc4-b4a3-3eeb0f09b4cc.jpg?strip=all&format=webp&crop=1600px%2C900px%2C0px%2C0px&resize=750)\n\n### I’ve lived on the French Riviera for years, these are my top restaurants in Nice\n\n![11 of the best restaurants in Barcelona](https://cms.thetimes.com/wp-content/uploads/image-server/production/0b77b209-2ece-4352-923e-1c32f497abb3.jpg?amp;sourceHeight=900&strip=all&format=webp&crop=0px%2C0px%2C1600px%2C900px&w=1328&resize=750)\n\n### 11 of the best restaurants in Barcelona\n\nPromoted Content\n\n![](/wp-content/plugins/tm-wp-frontend/assets/images/the-times-dual.webp)\n\n### Get in touch\n\n### More from The Times and The Sunday Times\n\n### More from News Corp\n\n© Times Media Limited 2025.  \nRegistered in England No. 894646. Registered office: 1 London Bridge Street, SE1 9GF.\n\n![](/wp-content/plugins/tm-wp-frontend/assets/images/ipso.webp)\n\n### The Flash Sale\n\n### £1 for 3 months\n\nEnjoy unlimited access to all articles.\nEnds soon.\n\n#### Continue reading\n\nEnjoy unlimited digital access.\n\nAlready have an account?\n[Log in ›](https://login.thetimes.com)",

      "title": "Is Krakow Expensive in 2026? A Real Daily Budget Breakdown",
      "content": "Street Food - Krakow’s street food is legendary and highly cost-effective. The obwarzanek krakowski, a ring-shaped bread sprinkled with salt, poppy seeds, or sesame, is a protected local speciality that costs as little as 3.50 PLN from the blue carts found on nearly every street corner. The zapiekanka, an open-faced baguette toasted with mushrooms and cheese, is the staple of the Plac Nowy in Kazimierz. In 2026, these large, filling snacks cost between 20 and 35 PLN and are frequently large enough to replace a full lunch or dinner. Located in Kazimierz, this establishment offers a \"Full Experience\" tasting menu for approximately 980 PLN.\n Craft Beer and Nightlife - Poland has emerged as a global leader in the craft beer movement. In 2026, a pint of high-quality local ale in a Kazimierz pub costs between 15 and 25 PLN.",

      "raw_content": "Follow us\n\n [Contact](/contact)\n\n[![English](https://dd-cdn.multiscreensite.com/flags/flags_iso/32/us.png) English en](/)\n\n[![Italiano](https://dd-cdn.multiscreensite.com/flags/flags_iso/32/it.png) Italiano it](/it)  [![Español](https://dd-cdn.multiscreensite.com/flags/flags_iso/32/es.png) Español es](/es)\n\n[BOOK NOW](/tours)\n\n[![Logo #hellocracow](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/20241212+LOGO+RIGHTS-1920w.png)](/)\n\n[![English](https://dd-cdn.multiscreensite.com/flags/flags_iso/32/us.png) English en](/)\n\n[![Italiano](https://dd-cdn.multiscreensite.com/flags/flags_iso/32/it.png) Italiano it](/it)  [![Español](https://dd-cdn.multiscreensite.com/flags/flags_iso/32/es.png) Español es](/es)\n\n[![Tripadvisor Travelers’ Choice Awards badge](https://static.tacdn.com/img2/travelers_choice/widgets/tchotel_2024_L.png)](https://www.tripadvisor.com/Attraction_Review-g274772-d12639539-Reviews-Hello_Cracow-\n  Krakow_Lesser_Poland_Province_Southern_Poland.html)\n\n[![Traveler Experiences logo](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/As+features+in+The+Travel+Guide+with+Telegraph-2-1920w.png)](https://magazine.natgeotraveller.co.uk/NGTCExp-Dec-2024/hello-cracow/)\n\n[![TripAdvisor Best of the Best 2025 badge](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/TA_2025-1920w.png)](https://www.tripadvisor.com/TravelersChoice-ThingsToDo)\n\n[![As featured in The Travel\n  Guide with Telegraph](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/As+features+in+The+Travel+Guide+with+Telegraph-4-1920w.png)](https://content.yudu.com/web/2q1af/0A33vvy/TELTG0326/html/index.html?page=25&origin=reader)\n\n### Is Krakow Expensive in 2026? A Real Daily Budget Breakdown\n\nHello Cracow • February 11, 2026\n\nKrakow remains an exceptionally affordable destination for international travelers in 2026, offering a high-standard European experience at a price point significantly lower than Western capitals like London, Paris, or Vienna. While global inflation has led to moderate price increases, the city continues to offer ca. 50% discounts on hospitality, dining, and cultural attractions compared to the UK or Scandinavia. A mid-range traveler can expect to spend approximately 350-500 PLN ($ 110–$140/£75–£100) per day, which covers boutique accommodation, a restaurant meal, and entry to major historical sites. The retention of the Polish Zloty (PLN) and high card acceptance across the city mean visitors can get the most from their spending while enjoying easy digital payments.\n\n| Category | Typical 2026 Pricing (PLN) | Best Time to Visit |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Budget Daily Total | 130 – 215 PLN | March (Lowest rates) |\n| Mid-Range Daily Total | 350 – 500 PLN | May–June / September |\n| Luxury Daily Total | 665 – 1,100+ PLN | December (Christmas) |\n| Obwarzanek (Bagel) | 3.50 – 5.00 PLN | Any time |\n| Zapiekanka (Street Food) | 20 – 35 PLN | Late night |\n| Milk Bar Meal | 15 – 30 PLN | Weekday lunch |\n| Auschwitz Guided Tour | 200 – 300 PLN | Year-round |\n| Salt Mine (Wieliczka) | 250 PLN | Year-round (14°C) |\n| 24-Hour Transport Pass | 17.00 PLN | Any time |\n\n![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/City+Center+Hello+Cracow-1920w.png)\n\n## The Economy of Krakow in 2026\n\nThe fiscal landscape of Krakow in 2026 is defined by a unique intersection of historical preservation and modern economic strategy. Despite the integration of the Polish economy within the European Union, the decision to maintain the Polish Złoty (PLN) rather than adopt the Euro serves as a primary driver for the city's affordability. This currency independence allows for lower labour costs and production expenses in the local hospitality sector, which translates into lower prices for international visitors.   \n\nComparative analysis suggests that a visitor will find their purchasing power nearly doubled in Krakow. For instance, while a mid-range dinner in Central London might cost £30 to £55, a comparable meal in Krakow’s Old Town averages between 60 and 80 PLN (approx. £12 to £18). This disparity is even more pronounced in the luxury sector, where five-star establishments in Krakow offer services at roughly ca. 30% of the cost of their counterparts in New York or Paris.\n\n#### Currency Management and Card Acceptance\n\nIn 2026, Krakow has achieved a state of near-total digital payment integration. Contactless payment is the standard, and it is accepted by almost every vendor, including small market stalls, souvenir shops in the Sukiennice, and the ubiquitous blue obwarzanek carts. Travelers will find that carrying significant amounts of cash is largely unnecessary.   \n\nHowever, the use of Polish Zloty (PLN) remains the bedrock of the local economy. When using international cards, it's recommended to choose the \"Pay in Local Currency\" option on card terminals to avoid the high fees associated with Dynamic Currency Conversion. While the Euro is occasionally accepted in larger hotels, the exchange rates offered are invariably inferior to the official interbank rate, making the use of PLN the most cost-effective strategy.\n\n![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Krakow+City+Hello+Cracow-1920w.png)\n\n## Detailed Daily Budget Categorisation\n\nThe 2026 travel market in Krakow caters to three distinct budgetary styles, each offering a high level of relative value.\n\n* **The Ultra-Budget Traveler (130 – 215 PLN/$30 – $50)** - A budget-conscious visitor can navigate the city effectively by utilizing the city’s extensive network of hostels and milk bars. This budget assumes accommodation in a dormitory bed, meals primarily from grocery stores or subsidized canteens, and a focus on the city’s numerous free attractions, such as the Main Market Square, the Planty Park, and the Vistula Boulevards.\n* **The Mid-Range Traveler (350 – 500 PLN/$110 – $140)** - The majority of visitors fall into this category, which reflects a balance of comfort and value. This budget accommodates a private room in a three-star hotel or a central apartment, dining in traditional Polish restaurants, and participating in at least one major guided excursion, such as a visit to the Wieliczka Salt Mine or Auschwitz-Birkenau. The mid-range experience in Krakow is frequently described as affordable luxury, as the quality of service and food often exceeds what is available at similar price points in Western Europe.\n* **The Luxury Traveler (665 – 1,100+ PLN/$155 – $250+)** - Luxury in Krakow is defined by high-end boutique hotels located in former aristocratic palaces and fine dining experiences that rival those of London or Paris at a fraction of the cost. This budget covers stays in five-star properties, private guided tours in electric golf carts, and multi-course tasting menus at restaurants.\n\n#### Accommodation Strategies Across Krakow’s Districts\n\nAccommodation is the most significant variable in a traveler’s budget, and in 2026, Krakow offers a diverse range of options situated within its historic districts. Unlike many European cities, where budget options are relegated to the outskirts, Krakow’s value-driven lodging is often located in the heart of the action.   \n\n* **Stare Miasto** - The Old Town remains the most popular choice for first-time visitors. In 2026, properties offer an immersion into medieval aesthetics. While this area commands the highest prices, travelers can still find exceptional value during the shoulder seasons (March–May and September–October), with boutique rooms occasionally dropping below $120 USD.\n* **Kazimierz** - The former Jewish district of Kazimierz has solidified its reputation as the city's centre for creative energy and nightlife. Accommodation here often features modern industrial design and integrated wellness facilities. This district is highly recommended for travelers who prioritize a vibrant evening scene and access to independent galleries and boutiques.\n* **Podgorze and Zablocie** - Located across the Father Bernatek Footbridge, Podgorze and Zablocie offer a more residential and authentic experience. This area is home to the Schindler’s Factory Museum and the MOCAK Museum of Contemporary Art. In 2026, these districts provide some of the city's best-value apartments and modern hotels, often at a 15% to 20% discount compared to the Old Town.\n\n#### Local Insight: The \"Hidden\" Fees of 2026\n\nA subtle but important logistical detail for 2026 is the Municipal Infrastructure Fee. While Poland does not have a formal tourist tax in the traditional sense, many short-term rental providers have implemented a fee of approximately €2.00 per person, per night. This fee is used to support urban infrastructure and historical preservation, and it is typically collected separately from the booking portal payment.   \n\nAdditionally, travelers must be aware of the Clean Transport Zone, which came into effect on January 1, 2026. This zone restricts the entry of older, high-emission vehicles into the city centre. For visitors who choose to drive, non-compliance with these environmental standards can result in high fines, making public transport or walking the preferred mode of navigation.\n\n![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Krakow+Hello+Cracow-c38be93b-1920w.png)\n\n#### Gastronomy: From Traditional Milk Bars to Michelin Stars\n\nThe culinary landscape of Krakow in 2026 is an arena where the Krakow advantage is most evident. The city provides a spectrum of dining options that allow for high-quality nutrition on any budget.   \n\n* **The Enduring Legacy of the Bar Mleczny** - For the budget traveler, the milk bar remains an essential cultural experience. These state-subsidized canteens offer traditional Polish dishes at prices that are accessible to all. In 2026, a hearty plate of dumplings or a bowl of sour rye soup costs between 15 and 30 PLN. Notable locations like Milkbar Tomasza or Bar Pod Temidą serve as a bridge between the communist past and the modern city, offering fresh, home-cooked food in a no-frills environment.\n* **Street Food** - Krakow’s street food is legendary and highly cost-effective. The obwarzanek krakowski, a ring-shaped bread sprinkled with salt, poppy seeds, or sesame, is a protected local speciality that costs as little as 3.50 PLN from the blue carts found on nearly every street corner. The zapiekanka, an open-faced baguette toasted with mushrooms and cheese, is the staple of the Plac Nowy in Kazimierz. In 2026, these large, filling snacks cost between 20 and 35 PLN and are frequently large enough to replace a full lunch or dinner.\n* **The Fine Dining Scene** - For those seeking a world-class culinary experience, Krakow hosts Poland’s only two-star Michelin restaurant, Bottiglieria 1881. Located in Kazimierz, this establishment offers a \"Full Experience\" tasting menu for approximately 980 PLN.\n* **Craft Beer and Nightlife** - Poland has emerged as a global leader in the craft beer movement. In 2026, a pint of high-quality local ale in a Kazimierz pub costs between 15 and 25 PLN.\n\n![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Main+Square+Hello+Cracow-1920w.png)\n\n## The 2026 Festival and Event Calendar\n\nThe timing of a visit significantly impacts both the atmosphere and the budget. In 2026, several major cultural events are scheduled to take place.   \n\n* **Easter 2026 (March 26 – April 6, 2026)** - The Easter Market in the Main Market Square is a highlight of the spring season. Visitors can purchase traditional handicrafts, hand-painted eggs, and regional foods. While the Wawel Castle exhibitions are closed on Easter Saturday and Sunday (April 4–5, 2026), the festive atmosphere and religious processions offer a unique cultural immersion.\n* **Juwenalia (May 13 – 23, 2026)** - This massive student festival transforms the city into a hub of outdoor concerts and parades. It is a time of high energy, and while it brings larger crowds to the city’s green spaces like the Błonia Park, it offers many free musical performances.\n* **Christmas Markets (November 28, 2026 – January 1, 2027)** - The Krakow Christmas Market is globally recognized for its festive charm. Historically, it begins on the last Friday of November. In 2026, visitors can enjoy mulled wine (grzaniec) and watch the traditional Szopka (nativity scene) competition, which is held on the first Thursday of December.\n\n![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Sukiennice+Hello+Cracow-1920w.png)\n\n## Planning Your 2026 Adventure\n\nKrakow remains an unparalleled destination in 2026, offering a sophisticated blend of tragedy and beauty, tradition and modernity, all while maintaining a cost of living that is highly advantageous for international visitors. By utilizing local insights, such as the value of milk bars and the strategic use of free museum days, a traveler can enjoy a luxury experience on a mid-range budget. The city does not attempt to \"polish\" its history but rather allows the medieval splendour of the Sukiennice to exist alongside the somber reminders of the 20th century in Podgorze. This authenticity, combined with world-class infrastructure and high-value pricing, makes Krakow the ultimate choice for a European city break in 2026.   \n\nReady to explore Krakow with the experts? At Hello Cracow, we specialize in providing authentic, sustainable, and locally-rooted tours that go beyond the typical tourist routes. From our high-standard Auschwitz-Birkenau excursions to our private electric golf cart city tours, we ensure every guest experiences the heart of the region with passion and professional insight.   \n\nBook your 2026 Krakow experience now with Hello Cracow and discover why we are the top-rated choice for travelers worldwide.\n\n [BOOK NOW](/tours)\n\n[< Older Post](/krakow-in-february-a-kaleidoscope-of-culture-and-celebration) [Newer Post >](/10-essential-holocaust-and-wwii-books)\n\n[![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Krakow+HelloCracow-3737ae3f-1920w.png)](/krakow-in-november-events-autumn-walks-and-what-to-book)\n\n### [Krakow In November: Events, Autumn Walks And What To Book](/krakow-in-november-events-autumn-walks-and-what-to-book)\n\nBy Hello Cracow • October 18, 2026\n\nKrakow in November is a city of earlier evenings. At four o'clock, the lamps are already on around Planty Park. The windows of cafes begin to glow, the Vistula is usually wrapped in mist, and a walk that felt straightforward at lunchtime suddenly calls for a warm table somewhere nearby. That can sound like a reason to wait for spring. In practice, it gives a short stay in Krakow a different rhythm. You do not try to spend twelve hours outdoors. You walk while there is light, choose one place worth lingering in, then let the evening belong to music, film, food or a museum that would have been too easy to skip in July.\n\n[![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Vistula+HelloCracow-1920w.png)](/what-should-you-book-before-visiting-krakow-a-no-stress-planning-checklist)\n\n### [What Should You Book Before Visiting Krakow? A No-Stress Planning Checklist](/what-should-you-book-before-visiting-krakow-a-no-stress-planning-checklist)\n\nBy Hello Cracow • October 6, 2026\n\nThere is a particular kind of Krakow trip that starts badly for a very ordinary reason. You arrive on Friday afternoon, leave your suitcase at the hotel, and open your phone to book Auschwitz for Sunday. The English tours are gone. Schindler's Factory has no tickets until Tuesday. The restaurant you saved on Instagram is fully booked. None of these problems will ruin the weekend, but suddenly the itinerary is being decided by whatever happens to be left. A little planning avoids that without turning a city break into a spreadsheet. Krakow still leaves plenty of room for long walks, unexpected cafes and evenings that continue because you like the neighbourhood. The important thing is knowing which experiences depend on a specific time, guide, language or number of available places. Think of your reservations as the frame around the trip, not the trip itself.\n\n[![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Tyniec+HelloCracow-ea6652b3-1920w.png)](/what-to-pack-for-krakow-in-autumn)\n\n### [What To Pack For Krakow In Autumn](/what-to-pack-for-krakow-in-autumn)\n\nBy Hello Cracow • October 5, 2026\n\nAutumn in Krakow often begins quietly. In September, you can still sit outside with a coffee near Main Market Square, walk through Planty Park in a light jacket and feel almost no urgency to leave the city before sunset. A few weeks later, the same streets look completely different. The chestnut trees drop their leaves, the light becomes softer, and an October morning that begins with clear skies can end with cold rain over Wawel Hill. ﻿ That change is exactly why Krakow is so rewarding in autumn. The city feels less hurried, cafes become part of the itinerary rather than a quick stop between attractions, and a warm dinner in Kazimierz feels deserved after a day on foot. Packing well means you can enjoy all of it without cutting a walk short because your shoes are wet or your jacket is still back at the hotel. The key is not packing for cold weather alone. It is packing for several versions of the same day.\n\n[![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Balice+Airport+HelloCracow-1920w.png)](/how-to-get-to-krakow-airports-trains-and-transfers-explained)\n\n### [How To Get To Krakow: Airports, Trains And Transfers Explained](/how-to-get-to-krakow-airports-trains-and-transfers-explained)\n\nBy Hello Cracow • September 28, 2026\n\nArriving in Krakow is usually straightforward, but the first hour can shape the whole trip. A late flight, heavy suitcase or unfamiliar ticket machine can quickly make a simple transfer feel more complicated than it needs to be. The good news is that Krakow is well connected by air and rail, and its main airport sits close enough to the city centre that getting to your hotel is usually fairly simple. ﻿ For most visitors, the choice comes down to Krakow Airport, the main railway station at Krakow Glowny, or a private transfer booked in advance. Each option works slightly differently, depending on what time you arrive, where you are staying and how much luggage you have. Public transport is often enough, but there are situations when paying for a direct transfer can make the first part of your trip much easier.\n\n[![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Krakow+Dawn+HelloCracow-1920w.png)](/my-post8d32c820)\n\n### [Krakow In October: A Kaleidoscope Of Culture And Celebration](/my-post8d32c820)\n\nBy Hello Cracow • September 23, 2026\n\nOctober is a great month to visit Krakow if you want to see the city without the summer crowds. The weather is usually still good enough for long walks, while cooler evenings make it easier to combine time outdoors with museums, restaurants and cafés. Planty Park starts to turn gold, the Old Town is less crowded, and Kazimierz is a good place to spend an afternoon without rushing from one sight to another. There is also plenty happening around the city. October brings concerts, film screenings, literary events, food festivals and other seasonal events, so it is worth checking what is on during your visit. You can easily combine one of these with the main sights, a walk along the Vistula or an evening in one of Krakow's neighbourhoods. It is also a good time to plan a day outside the city. Wieliczka, Auschwitz-Birkenau and Zakopane are all popular options, while the Tatra Mountains can be particularly appealing in autumn. Just keep an eye on the weather, as conditions outside Krakow can be quite different. ﻿ If you are planning a trip for October, you do not need to fill every day in advance. Pick a few things you definitely want to see, check the events happening during your dates and leave some time for whatever you find along the way.\n\n[![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Krakow+autumn+HelloCracow-1920w.png)](/what-to-pack-for-krakow-the-seasonal-guide-for-every-month)\n\n### [What To Pack For Krakow: The Seasonal Guide For Every Month](/what-to-pack-for-krakow-the-seasonal-guide-for-every-month)\n\nBy Hello Cracow • September 15, 2026\n\nKrakow is a city best explored on foot. A typical day can move from cobbled streets around the Old Town to the open riverside paths near Wawel, then on to Kazimierz or Podgorze, often with more walking than visitors expect. Even a simple plan can quickly turn into several hours outside, especially when you add a museum, a market, a café stop or one of the city’s many viewpoints. The right packing list starts with comfortable shoes, flexible layers and one piece of rain protection that fits in your day bag. Krakow weather changes quickly across the year, and sometimes within a single day. Spring can bring mild afternoons and sudden showers, while summer days can be warm enough for long walks but cooler once the sun goes down. Autumn tends to bring shorter days and more changeable conditions, while winter can mean cold temperatures, snow or wet streets. These differences matter when most of your day is spent moving between outdoor sights. The temperature ranges below are typical average lows and highs for Krakow-Balice, not a forecast for a particular trip. Conditions in the city centre can feel slightly different, especially on sunny days or when the wind picks up along the Vistula. Check the forecast again shortly before you travel, then pack for walking rather than for one perfect weather scenario. If you are planning trips outside Krakow, leave some room for further changes. Wieliczka is underground and noticeably cooler than the streets above, while a day in the Tatra Mountains can bring very different conditions from those in the city. A few simple choices can make it much easier to stay comfortable when your plans take you from one part of the region to another.\n\n[![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Birkenau+z+logo-1920w.png)](/voices-of-the-past-6-essential-polish-authors-to-read-before-visiting-auschwitz)\n\n### [Voices Of The Past: 6 Essential Polish Authors To Read Before Visiting Auschwitz](/voices-of-the-past-6-essential-polish-authors-to-read-before-visiting-auschwitz)\n\nBy Hello Cracow • September 7, 2026\n\nA visit to Auschwitz-Birkenau asks for more than simply adding another day trip to your Krakow itinerary. The former German Nazi concentration and extermination camp is a memorial site and a place of mass murder, where the scale of what happened can be difficult to understand through buildings, photographs and numbers alone. Reading before you go cannot prepare you for the visit itself, and it should not try to. What it can do is give some of the history a human dimension. Names, individual stories, memories and difficult choices become easier to recognise when you arrive at the site. Instead of knowing only what happened, you begin to understand something about the people who experienced it. The six writers below approach this history from very different perspectives. Four of them experienced Auschwitz as prisoners, writing about the camp from their own memories and experiences. Zofia Nalkowska encountered its aftermath from a different position, collecting testimonies and evidence about what had happened there in the months following the war. Hanna Krall offers yet another perspective. She survived the Holocaust as a child but was never imprisoned at Auschwitz. Her writing instead brings readers closer to the Jewish world destroyed during the Holocaust and to the people who carried its memory into the years that followed. There is no single book that can explain Auschwitz. Taken together, however, these works can help you arrive with more context and a greater awareness of the human stories behind the place.\n\n[![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Wawel+HelloCracow-387bbecf-1920w.png)](/the-best-quiet-places-in-krakow-when-the-old-town-gets-too-busy)\n\n### [The Best Quiet Places in Krakow When the Old Town Gets Too Busy](/the-best-quiet-places-in-krakow-when-the-old-town-gets-too-busy)\n\nBy Hello Cracow • August 30, 2026\n\nWhen the Main Market Square starts to feel a little too busy, you don't have to leave Krakow to find a different side of the city. Just beyond the usual sightseeing route, you'll find former quarries turned into green spaces, riverside paths, hilltop viewpoints, monastery gardens and neighbourhoods where life moves at a much slower pace. There is nothing wrong with spending your first day around Rynek Glowny, St Mary's Basilica, Wawel Castle and the Royal Route. They are famous for a reason, and for a first visit, they belong on the list. But after a few hours of following tour groups, waiting for a table and navigating busy streets, you may start looking for somewhere with a little more breathing room. That's when Krakow gets interesting. You don't need a complicated day trip or a long journey out of the city. Sometimes, a 15-minute tram ride or a short walk is enough to change the atmosphere completely. Suddenly there are more trees than souvenir shops, locals walking their dogs instead of organised groups, and paths where you can simply keep walking without having to stop every few metres. And these places aren't abandoned corners of the city. Krakow residents use them every day, particularly at weekends. You'll see people jogging, cycling, meeting friends, taking their children out or simply sitting somewhere with a coffee and enjoying the afternoon. So, if you're ready to put the guidebook down for a few hours, we've collected some of the best places to experience a quieter, greener and more local side of Krakow - all without straying too far from the city itself.\n\n[![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Tatry+HelloCracow-c71d6c7f-1920w.png)](/everything-you-need-to-know-about-a-tatra-quad-tour-from-krakow)\n\n### [Everything You Need To Know About A Tatra Quad Tour From Krakow](/everything-you-need-to-know-about-a-tatra-quad-tour-from-krakow)\n\nBy Hello Cracow • August 30, 2026\n\nLeave Krakow’s cobblestone streets behind and head south towards a completely different Poland. Beyond the city, the landscape opens into forests, meadows and mountain villages, with the Tatra peaks rising in the distance. A quad tour through Podhale gives you a chance to experience this landscape from the tracks rather than from a coach window. ﻿ The ride is the obvious highlight, but it is the journey that makes the day special. You move through quiet countryside, forest trails and highland landscapes, stopping along the way to take in the views and experience a slower side of southern Poland. Add a bonfire, local food or a soak in the thermal baths, depending on the tour, and a few hours outside Krakow can quickly turn into one of the most memorable days of your trip.\n\n[![](https://irp.cdn-website.com/702a94b7/dms3rep/multi/opt/Krakow+September+Hello+Cracow-1920w.png)](/krakow-in-september-a-local-guide-to-events-and-autumn-culture)\n\n### [Krakow in September: A Local Guide to Events and Autumn Culture](/krakow-in-september-a-local-guide-to-events-and-autumn-culture)\n\nBy Hello Cracow • August 17, 2026\n\nSeptember is when Krakow begins to soften into autumn. The warmest days still belong to the Vistula boulevards and café terraces, but Planty Park starts to change colour, and the city’s calendar becomes more local, varied and atmospheric. For first-time visitors, Krakow can seem centred entirely on the Main Market Square, Wawel Castle and Kazimierz. September is a good month to look beyond that first impression. A single weekend can bring music from Ireland and Scotland to a Renaissance manor house, local beekeepers to a historic Kazimierz square, and hundreds of dachshunds onto the Old Town’s most famous street. The city feels less like a checklist destination and more like a place where residents have their own rituals, neighbourhoods and reasons to gather. So, what is actually happening in Krakow this September? From the biggest cultural events to the smaller local traditions worth knowing about, here’s your guide to experiencing the city beyond the usual tourist highlights.\n\nMore Posts\n\n##### CONTACT INFO﻿\n\n[+48 500 720 706](tel:+48 500 720 706)\n\nPMC PARTNERS sp. z o.o.\n\nWadowicka 6, Krakow, 30-415, PL\n\ntax number: PL6793235208\n\n##### USEFUL LINKS\n\n##### FOLLOW US\n\nShare by:\n\n[Powered by Bókun](https://www.bokun.io/?utm_source=referral&utm_medium=bokun_widgets&utm_campaign=bokun_website)\n\n ",

      "title": "Krakow Travel Guide | Krakow Tourism - KAYAK",
      "content": "Shopping•Health & Beauty\n\nKlub Kombinator - bar with good vibes\n\nShopping•Food & Drink\n\nAxe throwing session in the oldest club in Europe\n\nSports & Fitness•Sports\n\nSyncret - Fine & Antique Jewellery\n\nShopping•Clothing & Accessories\n\nBunkier Sztuki Gallery of Contemporary Art\n\nArt & Performing Arts•Art Gallery\n\n## Where to Eat in Krakow\n\nFor cheap local food, look out for the Bar Mleczny milk bars. A meal here will cost around zł8. A meal in a mid-scale restaurant will cost around zł15-20 and a top restaurant like Wierzynek on the main square will cost upwards of zł30. U Babci Maliny is another quality restaurant, while Starka is a good choice for modern Polish food.\n\n## When to visit Krakow\n\nKrakow in August\n\nEstimated package price\n\n$71\n\n2 travelers\n\nFlight to Krakow\n\n3 nights at hotel [...] ### Popular Neighborhoods in Krakow\n\nThe Old Town - this is by far the most popular area in Krakow. This is where you will find most of the city's best-known attractions, including Wawel Castle, Wawel Cathedral, and Rynek Glowny. It is full of beautiful streets and squares and you can walk the Royal Way from the ancient city walls at St. Florian's Gate all the way to Wawel Castle.\n\nKazimierz - the city's Jewish Quarter was once a separate city to the south of Krakow, founded in 1335. It has many interesting museums and galleries along with synagogues and cemeteries to visit. It also has lots of atmospheric bars and restaurants serving affordable Jewish cuisine. [...] 5 Adults, 3 Bags\n\n$28/day\n\nStandard station wagonFind car rentals in Krakow\n\n## The Cost of Living in Krakow\n\n### Shopping Streets\n\nMost of the city's shopping is based in the Old Town, with many of the main stores around Rynek Glowny. You will find lots of antique stores, jewelry, and leather goods, and the Cloth Hall offers a fabulous shopping experience. Royal Way and Plac Nowy are also good locations.\n\n### Groceries and Other\n\nThere are two major shopping malls in the city center. Galeria Krakowska is next to the train station and Galeria Kazimierz is to the south and houses the upscale Alma supermarket. Everyday groceries can be bought from any number of local shops in the city center where a gallon of milk costs around zł10 and a loaf of bread zł2.20.\n\nCheap meal\n\nA pair of jeans",
      "score": 0.32376534,
      "raw_content": "What to do\n\nWhere to eat\n\nWhen to visit\n\nHow to get there\n\nWhere to stay\n\nRenting a car\n\nLocal costs\n\n# Krakow travel guide\n\n## Krakow Tourism | Krakow Guide\n\n## You're Going to Love Krakow\n\nAccording to the stories, Krakow was founded by defeating a dragon and today's city reflects something of that dramatic birth.\n\n![Article](https://content.r9cdn.net/rimg/dimg/41/43/9531cd74-city-9856-16f6711c97a.jpg?crop=true&width=1020&height=498)\n\nThis is one of the most beautiful cities in Europe and, largely undamaged in World War II, it has one of the best-preserved city centers. Indeed, the ancient architecture is one of the main attractions.\n\nThe Old Town features many soaring church spires and fascinating museums, along with the largest market square in Europe, Rynek Glowny. Kazimierz is the atmospheric Jewish Quarter, soaked in history and bustling with bars and restaurants.\n\nThe city also buzzes with bars and nightlife and the diversity of a university town. It's also an easily walkable city with plenty of charming backstreets and alleyways in which to get pleasantly lost.\n\n## Top 5 Reasons to Visit Krakow\n\n### 1. Exploring Wawel Castle\n\nWawel Castle dominates the Old Town from its lofty position on the hill overlooking the River Vistula to the south. It is widely thought to be one of the most beautiful castles in Europe and dates from the 16th century. You can walk the grounds for free, but the five museums and exhibitions all require separate entry tickets.\n\n### 2. Browse Rynek Glowny\n\nKrakow's main square has been the center of city life for some 700 years. Surrounded by bars and restaurants, it is a wonderful place to eat, drink, and soak up the atmosphere. At the center of the square is the fabulous Cloth Hall, thought to be the oldest covered shopping mall in the world.\n\n### 3. Dive Deep into Rynek Underground\n\nRynek Glowny is impressive enough but by going underground you can get a completely different perspective on the square. Ancient tunnels and medieval market stalls are brought to life by holograms and audiovisual displays.\n\n### 4. Schindler's Factory\n\nThis fascinating museum covers the time of Nazi occupation of Krakow in World War II. It is sited in the enamel factory once owned by Oskar Schindler, whose story was told in the film Schindler's List.\n\n### 5. Wieliczka Salt Mine\n\nJust 10 miles southeast of Krakow, the Wieliczka Salt Mine is a popular day trip. A vast labyrinth of tunnels and chambers has been carved from the salt, and it makes for a fascinating guided tour.\n\n### 1. Exploring Wawel Castle\n\nWawel Castle dominates the Old Town from its lofty position on the hill overlooking the River Vistula to the south. It is widely thought to be one of the most beautiful castles in Europe and dates from the 16th century. You can walk the grounds for free, but the five museums and exhibitions all require separate entry tickets.\n\n### 2. Browse Rynek Glowny\n\nKrakow's main square has been the center of city life for some 700 years. Surrounded by bars and restaurants, it is a wonderful place to eat, drink, and soak up the atmosphere. At the center of the square is the fabulous Cloth Hall, thought to be the oldest covered shopping mall in the world.\n\n### 3. Dive Deep into Rynek Underground\n\nRynek Glowny is impressive enough but by going underground you can get a completely different perspective on the square. Ancient tunnels and medieval market stalls are brought to life by holograms and audiovisual displays.\n\n### 4. Schindler's Factory\n\nThis fascinating museum covers the time of Nazi occupation of Krakow in World War II. It is sited in the enamel factory once owned by Oskar Schindler, whose story was told in the film Schindler's List.\n\n### 5. Wieliczka Salt Mine\n\nJust 10 miles southeast of Krakow, the Wieliczka Salt Mine is a popular day trip. A vast labyrinth of tunnels and chambers has been carved from the salt, and it makes for a fascinating guided tour.\n\n## What to do in Krakow\n\n### 1. Stare Miasto: The Oldest Part of Town\n\nEncircled by lush parklands, the old city center is home to some of Krakow's greatest sights. Start at the medieval Main Square, one of the largest in Europe. Visit both St. Mary's Basilica and the 13th-century Cloth Hall market - now a beautiful museum. Just outside you will find the Adam Mickiewicz Monument, and for some context, the Historical Museum of Kraków. Wander the surrounding streets until you reach St. Florian's Gate, which once defended the entrance to this ancient part of town.\n\n### 2. Wawel Castle: Medieval and Majestic\n\nAt the edge of Stare Miasto, this age-old castle has overlooked the Vistula River since the Middle Ages. One of the country's most culturally significant attractions, the fortress was a long time home to Polish monarchs before becoming a world-class museum of art. Visit the Crown treasury and armory to immerse yourself in dazzling artifacts of priceless historical value, and wander outside to take in the equally precious cityscape across the water. Royal gardens await for a delightful stroll too.\n\n### 3. Muzeum Książąt Czartoryskich: Temple of Memory\n\nEstablished for the preservation of Polish heritage by Princess Izabela Czartoryska, the collection in this museum is a treasure trove. Each artwork sheds light on the history of the country meaning that a journey through the gallery is both aesthetic and enlightening. Masterpieces like Leonardo da Vinci's \"Lady with an Ermine\" can be admired for hours, the skill unparalleled, while priceless works of art stolen during World War II are still sorely missed - a fascinating history.\n\n### 4. Kopalnia soli Wieliczka: Underground Salt Cathedral\n\nUntil 2007, this 13th-century mine actively produced table salt in what can only be described as an underground metropolis. Since it opened as a tourist attraction, visitors have been mind-blown by the elaborate subterranean network. A tour takes you through 2 miles of the labyrinth, a mere fraction of the vast space, yet surprises await at every turn. Chandeliers of salt-crystals, enormous chapels, and incredible carvings speckle the caves - it's like nothing you've ever seen before.\n\n### 5. Kazimierz: Reflect on the Centuries\n\nAlong the Vistula River, this time-worn neighborhood is both charming and historic. Visit the gothic-style Corpus Christi Basilica to explore one of the oldest churches in the city, and wander onwards to discover the Jewish heritage of the quarter. Synagogues abound, and the Old Synagogue provides context about the neighborhood with its Jewish History Museum. Remah Cemetery bears generations of history, and to commemorate Jewish victims of the Holocaust; you can also consider a day trip to Auschwitz.\n\n![Stare Miasto](https://content.r9cdn.net/rimg/dimg/cd/e7/7e96d54c-lm-4326-16c4882758e.jpg?width=322&height=200&crop=true)![Stare Miasto](https://content.r9cdn.net/rimg/dimg/cd/e7/7e96d54c-lm-4326-16c4882758e.jpg?width=688&height=350&crop=true)\n\n![Wawel Castle](https://content.r9cdn.net/rimg/dimg/41/43/9531cd74-city-9856-16f6711b3fc.jpg?width=151&height=240&crop=true)![Wawel Castle](https://content.r9cdn.net/rimg/dimg/41/43/9531cd74-city-9856-16f6711b3fc.jpg?width=334&height=380&crop=true)\n\n![Muzeum Książąt Czartoryskich](https://content.r9cdn.net/rimg/dimg/7b/f4/e34d2903-lm-13678-173552f8c07.jpg?width=151&height=240&crop=true)![Muzeum Książąt Czartoryskich](https://content.r9cdn.net/rimg/dimg/7b/f4/e34d2903-lm-13678-173552f8c07.jpg?width=334&height=380&crop=true)\n\n### 1. A meal here will cost around zł8. A meal in a mid-scale restaurant will cost around zł15-20 and a top restaurant like Wierzynek on the main square will cost upwards of zł30. U Babci Maliny is another quality restaurant, while Starka is a good choice for modern Polish food.\n\n## When to visit Krakow\n\nKrakow in August\n\nEstimated package price\n\n$71\n\n2 travelers\n\nFlight to Krakow\n\n3 nights at hotel\n\nKrakow in August\n\nEstimated package price\n\n$71\n\n2 travelers\n\nFlight to Krakow\n\n3 nights at hotel\n\nKrakow has four very distinct seasons, so you can choose the sort of weather that suits you. Summers are humid and hot, with temperatures reaching 85 degrees Fahrenheit or more. Winters are cold and snowy, with temperatures as low as 5 degrees Fahrenheit not uncommon. Spring and fall are mild and are perhaps best-suited to comfortable walking in the city.\n\nData provided by[![weatherbase](/res/images/horizon/hotels/cityguides/weatherbase-logo.png)](#)\n\nTemperatures\n\nTemperatures\n\nAverage\n\nFahrenheit (°F)\n\nData provided by[![weatherbase](/res/images/horizon/hotels/cityguides/weatherbase-logo.png)](#)\n\n## How to Get to Krakow\n\n### Plane\n\nKrakow Airport (KRK), also known as John Paul II International Airport, is the city's main airport and is located nine miles west of Krakow in Balice. Buses leave every 20 minutes from the airport to Krakow city center. The journey takes around 40 minutes and costs zł4. There is also a rail link into the city, which leaves every 30 minutes, takes 20 minutes, and costs zł8. Taxi ranks can be found in front of both Terminal 1 and 2, but the fare is around zł89.\n\n### Train\n\nThe main train station in Krakow is Dworzec Glowny PKP, and it is located just beside the Old Town. There are good connections to other Polish cities including Warsaw. A ticket for the 2 hour 20 minutes journey bought on the day could cost zł120 but drops to zł49 if bought in advance. Overnight international sleeper services connect the city to Budapest, Prague, and Vienna. If you don't speak Polish, buy tickets at the international ticket counter.\n\n### Car\n\nThe new A4 motorway has recently been completed, connecting Krakow to the German border and the Autobahn A4. This has greatly improved car travel west to Germany. The toll is zł18. The main route to Warsaw is the E77, which takes just under five hours.\n\n### Bus\n\nMany companies operate international bus services to Krakow. Eurolines connects many Western European countries with the city, including France, Italy, the UK, and Scandinavia. Ecolines is the main operator serving connections to the Baltic States, Russia, and Central and Eastern Europe.\n\n### Plane\n\nKrakow Airport (KRK), also known as John Paul II International Airport, is the city's main airport and is located nine miles west of Krakow in Balice. These areas are convenient for most of the city's sights and attractions, and make it easy to walk around the relatively compact city center. Aparthotel Stare Miasto offers luxury accommodation just a few steps from the main square, while the Senacki Hotel provides quality rooms on the Royal Way between the square and Wawel Castle.\n\n### Popular Neighborhoods in Krakow\n\n**The Old Town** - this is by far the most popular area in Krakow. This is where you will find most of the city's best-known attractions, including Wawel Castle, Wawel Cathedral, and Rynek Glowny. It is full of beautiful streets and squares and you can walk the Royal Way from the ancient city walls at St. Florian's Gate all the way to Wawel Castle.\n\n**Kazimierz** - the city's Jewish Quarter was once a separate city to the south of Krakow, founded in 1335. It has many interesting museums and galleries along with synagogues and cemeteries to visit. It also has lots of atmospheric bars and restaurants serving affordable Jewish cuisine.\n\n**The Western District** - this is the city suburb nearest the center and actually surrounds the Old Town and much of Kazimierz. It is known as the greenest area of Krakow and includes the Las Wolski forest.\n\n**The Old Town** - this is by far the most popular area in Krakow. This is where you will find most of the city's best-known attractions, including Wawel Castle, Wawel Cathedral, and Rynek Glowny. It has many interesting museums and galleries along with synagogues and cemeteries to visit. It also has lots of atmospheric bars and restaurants serving affordable Jewish cuisine.\n\n**The Western District** - this is the city suburb nearest the center and actually surrounds the Old Town and much of Kazimierz. It is known as the greenest area of Krakow and includes the Las Wolski forest.\n\nMost popular hotel in Krakow by neighborhood\n\n## Where to stay in popular areas of Krakow\n\n## Most booked hotels in Krakow\n\n[See all](/Krakow-Hotels.9856.hotel.ksp)\n\nHampton by Hilton Krakow Airport\n\n3 stars\n\nExcellent (9.1, 7762 reviews)\n\n$103+\n\n[View](/Balice-Hotels-Hampton-by-Hilton-Krakow-Airport.8132590.ksp)\n\nTurowka Hotel & Spa\n\n4 stars\n\nExcellent (9.1, 2286 reviews)\n\n$101+\n\n[View](/Wieliczka-Hotels-Turowka-Hotel-Spa.350898.ksp)\n\nHilton Garden Inn Krakow Airport\n\n4 stars\n\nExcellent (9, 6409 reviews)\n\n$147+\n\n[View](/Balice-Hotels-Hilton-Garden-Inn-Krakow-Airport.800492.ksp)\n\nHotel Vinnica\n\n3 stars\n\nExcellent (9, 483 reviews)\n\n$82+\n\n[View](/Wieliczka-Hotels-Hotel-Vinnica.3522139.ksp)\n\nHyatt Place Krakow\n\n4 stars\n\nExcellent (8.8, 3424 reviews)\n\n$86+\n\n[View](/Krakow-Hotels-Hyatt-Place-Krakow.8845051.ksp)\n\nNovotel Krakow City West\n\n4 stars\n\nExcellent (8.7, 3577 reviews)\n\n$70+\n\n[View](/Krakow-Hotels-Novotel-Krakow-City-West.59470.ksp)\n\n## How to Get Around Krakow\n\n### Public Transportation\n\nKrakow is well-served by public transport, with an extensive bus and tram network. Traffic can be slow in the rush hours between 7-9 am and 3-5 pm, so expect some delays at these times. There are lots of ticket machines at tram and bus stops and in nearby kiosks. These provide instructions in English. There are also ticket machines on some of the buses and trams themselves and these vehicles have an 'A' sign in the doorway. If you buy a ticket from the driver you will have to pay zł5 and give him the exact money. No change is provided. In all cases you need to validate your ticket by punching it in the machine as soon as you get on. Tickets range from zł2.80 for 20 minutes to zł15 for 24 hours.\n\n### Taxi\n\nTaxis in Krakow are generally fair and reliable. A typical daytime fare around the city will be around zł20. This is made up of an initial fee of zł5-7 and then zł1.25-1.8 per mile. Official taxis have a sign on the roof and a price list on the passenger door.\n\n### Car\n\nDriving a car in Krakow is not recommended. Traffic can be heavy and Polish driving customs can be a little challenging. Parking in the city center is expensive and hard to come by and 'driving zone' rules confuse even local residents. If you do hire a car, expect to pay upwards of zł100 per day, and Avis and Sixt outlets can be found in the city.\n\n### Public Transportation\n\nKrakow is well-served by public transport, with an extensive bus and tram network. Traffic can be slow in the rush hours between 7-9 am and 3-5 pm, so expect some delays at these times. There are lots of ticket machines at tram and bus stops and in nearby kiosks. These provide instructions in English. There are also ticket machines on some of the buses and trams themselves and these vehicles have an 'A' sign in the doorway. If you buy a ticket from the driver you will have to pay zł5 and give him the exact money. No change is provided. In all cases you need to validate your ticket by punching it in the machine as soon as you get on. Tickets range from zł2.80 for 20 minutes to zł15 for 24 hours.\n\n### Taxi\n\nTaxis in Krakow are generally fair and reliable. A typical daytime fare around the city will be around zł20. If you do hire a car, expect to pay upwards of zł100 per day, and Avis and Sixt outlets can be found in the city.\n\n#### Best car rental deals in Krakow\n\n[Intermediate SUV\n\n5 Adults, 3 Bags\n\n$13/day\n\n![Intermediate SUV]()](/Alamo-Car-Rentals-Krakow.AALAMO.9856.cl.ksp)[Standard station wagon\n\n5 Adults, 3 Bags\n\n$28/day\n\n![Standard station wagon]()](/Enterprise-Car-Rentals-Krakow.AENTERPRISE.9856.cl.ksp)[Find car rentals in Krakow](/Cheap-Krakow-Car-Rentals.9856.cars.ksp)\n\n## The Cost of Living in Krakow\n\n### Shopping Streets\n\nMost of the city's shopping is based in the Old Town, with many of the main stores around Rynek Glowny. You will find lots of antique stores, jewelry, and leather goods, and the Cloth Hall offers a fabulous shopping experience. Royal Way and Plac Nowy are also good locations.\n\n### Groceries and Other\n\nThere are two major shopping malls in the city center. Galeria Krakowska is next to the train station and Galeria Kazimierz is to the south and houses the upscale Alma supermarket. Everyday groceries can be bought from any number of local shops in the city center where a gallon of milk costs around zł10 and a loaf of bread zł2.20.\n\nCheap meal\n\nA pair of jeans\n\n$4.40\n\nSingle public transport ticket\n\n## Everything you need to know for your stay in Krakow\n\nPopular cities\n\n### Other popular cities in Poland\n\nWelcome to KAYAK\n\nThis site uses cookies and other tracking technologies for essential, functional, analytical, and advertising purposes.       "content": "Kraków, officially the Royal Capital City of Kraków, is the second-largest and one of the oldest cities in Poland. Situated on the Vistula River in Lesser Poland Voivodeship, the city has a population of 816,614 (2025), with 1,428,363 people living in the Kraków metropolitan area. Kraków was the official capital of Poland until 1596 and has traditionally been one of the leading centres of Polish economic, academic, cultural, and artistic life. Its Old Town was declared a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1978, one of the world's first sites granted the status. \"Polish language\")) |\n| GDP [[3]](#cite_note-ec.europa.eu-3)[[4]](#cite_note-4) |\n| • City | €26.227 billion (2023) |\n| • [Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_metropolitan_area \"Kraków metropolitan area\") | €28.742 billion (2021) |\n| [Time zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_zone \"Time zone\") | [UTC+1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTC+1 \"UTC+1\") Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Site \"World Heritage Site\") |\n| Official name | [Historic Centre of Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_Centre_of_Kraków \"Historic
