|\n\n**Kraków**,[[a]](#cite_note-9) officially the **Royal Capital City of Kraków**,[[b]](#cite_note-12) is the [second-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Poland \"List of cities and towns in Poland\") and one of the oldest cities in [Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poland \"Poland\"). Situated on the [Vistula River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vistula_River \"Vistula River\") in [Lesser Poland Voivodeship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesser_Poland_Voivodeship \"Lesser Poland Voivodeship\"), the city has a population of 816,614 (2025), with 1,428,363 people living in the [Kraków metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_metropolitan_area \"Kraków metropolitan area\").[[11]](#cite_note-welcome-13)[[12]](#cite_note-14) Kraków was the official capital of Poland until 1596[[13]](#cite_note-15) and has traditionally been one of the leading centres of Polish economic, academic, cultural, and artistic life. [Its Old Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Old_Town \"Kraków Old Town\") was declared a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_World_Heritage_Site \"UNESCO World Heritage Site\") in 1978, one of the world's first sites granted the status.\n\nThe city began as a [fortified settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gord_(archaeology) \"Gord (archaeology)\") on Wawel Hill around the 8th century.[[14]](#cite_note-History-16) In 1038, it became the seat of [Polish monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Poland \"King of Poland\") from the [Piast dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piast_dynasty \"Piast dynasty\"), and subsequently served as the centre of administration under [Jagiellonian kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagiellonian_dynasty \"Jagiellonian dynasty\") and of the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish–Lithuanian_Commonwealth \"Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth\") until the late 16th century, when [Sigismund III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_III \"Sigismund III\") transferred his [royal court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_court \"Royal court\") to [Warsaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw \"Warsaw\"). With the emergence of the [Second Polish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Polish_Republic \"Second Polish Republic\") in 1918, Kraków reaffirmed its role as the nucleus of a national spirit. After the [invasion of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland \"Invasion of Poland\"), at the start of [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II \"World War II\"), the newly defined *[Distrikt Krakau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distrikt_Krakau \"Distrikt Krakau\")* became the seat of [Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany \"Nazi Germany\")'s [General Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Government \"General Government\"). The Jewish population was forced into the [Kraków Ghetto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Ghetto \"Kraków Ghetto\"), a walled zone from where they were sent to Nazi [extermination camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extermination_camp \"Extermination camp\") such as the nearby [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz \"Auschwitz\"), and [Nazi concentration camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_concentration_camps \"Nazi concentration camps\") like [Płaszów](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków-Płaszów_concentration_camp \"Kraków-Płaszów concentration camp\").[[15]](#cite_note-ARC-17) However, the city was spared from destruction. In 1978, [Karol Wojtyła](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karol_Wojtyła \"Karol Wojtyła\"), [archbishop of Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archbishop_of_Kraków \"Archbishop of Kraków\"), was elevated to the [papacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papacy \"Papacy\") as Pope John Paul II, the first non-Italian pope in 455 years.[[16]](#cite_note-Clark-18)\n\nThe Old Town and historic centre of Kraków, along with the nearby [Wieliczka Salt Mine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wieliczka_Salt_Mine \"Wieliczka Salt Mine\"), are Poland's first [World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Site \"World Heritage Site\").[[17]](#cite_note-Centre-19)[[18]](#cite_note-unesco-02com-20) Its extensive cultural and architectural legacy across the epochs of [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture \"Gothic architecture\"), [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_architecture \"Renaissance architecture\"), and [Baroque architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_architecture \"Baroque architecture\") includes [Wawel Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Cathedral \"Wawel Cathedral\") and [Wawel Royal Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Royal_Castle \"Wawel Royal Castle\") on the banks of the Vistula, [St. Mary's Basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_Assumption_of_the_Blessed_Virgin_Mary,_Kraków \"Church of the Assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary, Kraków\"), [Saints Peter and Paul Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saints_Peter_and_Paul_Church,_Kraków \"Saints Peter and Paul Church, Kraków\"), and the largest [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval \"Medieval\") market square in Europe, *[Rynek Główny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Square,_Kraków \"Main Square, Kraków\")*.[[19]](#cite_note-21) Kraków is home to [Jagiellonian University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagiellonian_University \"Jagiellonian University\"), one of the [oldest universities in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_oldest_universities_in_continuous_operation \"List of oldest universities in continuous operation\") and often considered Poland's most reputable academic institution of higher learning. The city also hosts a number of institutions of national significance, including the [National Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum,_Kraków \"National Museum, Kraków\"), [Kraków Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Opera \"Kraków Opera\"), [Juliusz Słowacki Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juliusz_Słowacki_Theatre \"Juliusz Słowacki Theatre\"), [National Stary Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Stary_Theatre \"National Stary Theatre\"), and the [Jagiellonian Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagiellonian_Library \"Jagiellonian Library\").\n\nKraków is classified as a [global city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_city \"Global city\") with the ranking of \"high sufficiency\" by the [Globalization and World Cities Research Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization_and_World_Cities_Research_Network \"Globalization and World Cities Research Network\").[[20]](#cite_note-22) The city is served by [John Paul II International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Paul_II_International_Airport \"John Paul II International Airport\"), the country's second busiest airport and the most important international airport for the inhabitants of south-eastern Poland. In 2000, Kraków was named [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture \"European Capital of Culture\"). In 2013, Kraków was officially approved as a [UNESCO City of Literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_City_of_Literature \"UNESCO City of Literature\").[[21]](#cite_note-23) The city hosted [World Youth Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Youth_Day \"World Youth Day\") in 2016,[[22]](#cite_note-24) and the [European Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_European_Games \"2023 European Games\") in 2023.[[23]](#cite_note-25)\n\n## Etymology\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=1 \"Edit section: Etymology\")]\n\nSee also: [Names of Kraków in different languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_European_cities_in_different_languages:_I-L#K \"Names of European cities in different languages: I-L\")\n\nThe name of Kraków is traditionally derived from [Krakus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krakus \"Krakus\") (Krak, Grakch), the legendary founder of Kraków and a ruler of the [early Medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Medieval \"Early Medieval\") tribe of [Vistulans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vistulans \"Vistulans\").[[24]](#cite_note-Nungovitch1-26) In Polish, *Kraków* is an [archaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Polish \"Old Polish\") [possessive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Possessive_case \"Possessive case\") form of *Krak* and essentially means \"Krak's (town)\".[[25]](#cite_note-Małecki-27) The true origin of the name is highly disputed among historians, with many theories in existence and no unanimous consensus.[[24]](#cite_note-Nungovitch1-26) The first recorded mention of Prince Krakus (then written as *Grakch*) dates back to 1190, although the town existed as early as the seventh century, when it was inhabited by the tribe of Vistulans.[[14]](#cite_note-History-16) It is possible that the name of the city is derived from the word *[kruk](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/kruk#Polish \"wikt:kruk\")*, meaning 'crow' or 'raven'.[[26]](#cite_note-28)\n\nThe city's full official name is *Stołeczne Królewskie Miasto Kraków*,[[27]](#cite_note-bip.krakow-UCHWAŁA-29) which can be translated as \"[Royal Capital City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_city \"Royal city\") of Kraków\". In English, a person born or living in Kraków is a Cracovian ([Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language \"Polish language\"): *krakowianin* or *krakus*).[[28]](#cite_note-Tyrmand-30) Until the 2000s, the English form of the name *Cracow* substantially led in English prevalence the recent and less-modified borrowings *Krakow* and *Kraków*. However, currently the most common variant in English text is the unmodified *Kraków*.[[29]](#cite_note-31)\n\nForeign names include [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language \"German language\") and [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language \"Dutch language\") *Krakau*; [Yiddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish \"Yiddish\") קראָקע‎ (*Krake*); [Czech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Czech_language \"Czech language\") and [Slovak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slovak_language \"Slovak language\") *Krakov*; [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language \"Latin language\"), [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_language \"Italian language\"), [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language \"Spanish language\") and [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_language \"Romanian language\") *Cracovia*;[[30]](#cite_note-32) [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language \"Portuguese language\") *Cracóvia*; [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language \"French language\") *Cracovie*; [Hungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_language \"Hungarian language\") *Krakkó*; [Lithuanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_language \"Lithuanian language\") *Krokuva*; [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language \"Ukrainian language\") Краків (*Krakiv*); and [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language \"Russian language\") Краков (*Krakov*).[[31]](#cite_note-krakow5-33)\n\n## History\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=2 \"Edit section: History\")]\n\nMain article: [History of Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Kraków \"History of Kraków\")\n\nFor a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Kraków \"Timeline of Kraków\").\n\n### Origins and Middle Ages\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=3 \"Edit section: Origins and Middle Ages\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Wawel_St_Leonard_ed.jpg/250px-Wawel_St_Leonard_ed.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Wawel_St_Leonard_ed.jpg)\n\nThe [Romanesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanesque_architecture \"Romanesque architecture\") [St. Leonard's Crypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Leonard's_Crypt \"St. Leonard's Crypt\") dates back to the 11th century, when [Casimir I the Restorer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_I_the_Restorer \"Casimir I the Restorer\") made Kraków his royal residence and the capital of the [Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Poland \"Kingdom of Poland\").\n\nThe earliest evidence human settlement in Kraków comes from the [Stone Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_Age \"Stone Age\") on the present site of the [Wawel Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Hill \"Wawel Hill\").[[32]](#cite_note-Fischinger-34)\n\nKraków began as a fortified settlement of the [Vistulans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vistulans \"Vistulans\"), a [Lechitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lechites \"Lechites\") tribe, around the 8th century. A legend attributes Kraków's founding to the mythical ruler [Krakus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krakus \"Krakus\"), who built it above a cave occupied by a [dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon \"Dragon\"), [Smok Wawelski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smok_Wawelski \"Smok Wawelski\"). The first written record of the city's name dates back to 965, when Kraków was described as a notable commercial centre controlled by Moravia (876–879), but captured by a Bohemian duke [Boleslaus I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boleslaus_I_of_Bohemia \"Boleslaus I of Bohemia\") in 955.[[33]](#cite_note-krakow.pl-2-35) The first acclaimed ruler of Poland, [Mieszko I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mieszko_I \"Mieszko I\"), took Kraków from the Bohemians and incorporated it into the holdings of the [Piast dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piast_dynasty \"Piast dynasty\") towards the end of his reign.[[34]](#cite_note-Živković-36)\n\nIn 1038, Kraków became the seat of the Polish government.[[14]](#cite_note-History-16) By the end of the tenth century, the city was a leading centre of trade.[[35]](#cite_note-Van_Dongen-37) Brick buildings were constructed, including the Royal [Wawel Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Castle \"Wawel Castle\") with St. Felix and Adaukt Rotunda, [Romanesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanesque_architecture \"Romanesque architecture\") churches such as [St. Andrew's Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Andrew's_Church,_Kraków \"St. Andrew's Church, Kraków\"), [a cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Cathedral \"Wawel Cathedral\"), and [a basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Mary's_Basilica,_Kraków \"St. Mary's Basilica, Kraków\").[[36]](#cite_note-Rosik_-_Urbańczyk-38) [The city was sacked and burned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sack_of_Kraków_(1241) \"Sack of Kraków (1241)\") during the [Mongol invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Mongol_invasion_of_Poland \"First Mongol invasion of Poland\") of 1241.[[37]](#cite_note-39) It was rebuilt practically identically,[[38]](#cite_note-Akt_lokacyjny-40) based on new location act and [incorporated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_corporation \"Municipal corporation\") in 1257 by the high duke [Bolesław V the Chaste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolesław_V_the_Chaste \"Bolesław V the Chaste\") who following the example of [Wrocław](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrocław \"Wrocław\"), introduced city rights modelled on the [Magdeburg law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magdeburg_law \"Magdeburg law\") allowing for tax benefits and new trade privileges for the citizens.[[39]](#cite_note-Strzala2-41) In 1259, the city was [again ravaged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Mongol_invasion_of_Poland \"Second Mongol invasion of Poland\") by the Mongols. A [third attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Mongol_invasion_of_Poland \"Third Mongol invasion of Poland\") in 1287 was repelled thanks in part to the [newly built fortifications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Florian's_Gate \"St. Florian's Gate\").[[40]](#cite_note-Kolodziejczyk-42) In 1315 a large alliance of Poland, Denmark, [Norway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Norway_(872–1397) \"Kingdom of Norway (872–1397)\") and [Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Sweden_(800–1521) \"Kingdom of Sweden (800–1521)\") was formed in Kraków.[[41]](#cite_note-43)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Nuremberg_chronicles_-_CRACOVIA.png/250px-Nuremberg_chronicles_-_CRACOVIA.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nuremberg_chronicles_-_CRACOVIA.png)\n\nWoodcut of Kraków from the [Nuremberg Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg_Chronicle \"Nuremberg Chronicle\"), 1493\n\nIn 1335, King [Casimir III the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_III_the_Great \"Casimir III the Great\") ([Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language \"Polish language\"): *Kazimierz*) declared the two western suburbs to be a new city named after him, [Kazimierz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazimierz \"Kazimierz\") ([Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language \"Latin language\"): *Casimiria*). The defensive walls were erected around the central section of Kazimierz in 1362, and a plot was set aside for the [Augustinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustinians \"Augustinians\") order next to [Skałka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skałka \"Skałka\").[[42]](#cite_note-Świszczowski-44) The city rose to prominence in 1364, when Casimir founded the [University of Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagiellonian_University \"Jagiellonian University\"),[[43]](#cite_note-The_establishment_of_a_university-45) the second oldest university in central Europe after the [Charles University in Prague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_University_in_Prague \"Charles University in Prague\").\n\nThe city continued to grow under the [Jagiellonian dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagiellonian_dynasty \"Jagiellonian dynasty\"). As the capital of the [Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Poland_(1385–1569) \"Kingdom of Poland (1385–1569)\") and a member of the [Hanseatic League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanseatic_League \"Hanseatic League\"), the city attracted many craftsmen from abroad,[[44]](#cite_note-46) businesses, and [guilds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_guilds \"Medieval guilds\") as science and the arts began to flourish.[[45]](#cite_note-poloniahans-47) The royal chancery and the university ensured a first flourishing of Polish literary culture in the city.[[46]](#cite_note-48)\n\n### Early modern period\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=4 \"Edit section: Early modern period\")]\n\nThe 15th and 16th centuries were known as Poland's *Złoty Wiek* or [Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_age_(metaphor) \"Golden age (metaphor)\").[[47]](#cite_note-NormanDavies-49) Many works of [Polish Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Renaissance \"Polish Renaissance\") art and architecture were created,[[48]](#cite_note-Mikos-50)[[49]](#cite_note-unescoancient-51) including synagogues in Kraków's Jewish quarter located in the north-eastern part of Kazimierz, such as the [Old Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Synagogue_(Kraków) \"Old Synagogue (Kraków)\").[[50]](#cite_note-infosyn-52) During the reign of [Casimir IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_IV_Jagiellon \"Casimir IV Jagiellon\"), various artists came to work and live in Kraków, and [Johann Haller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Haller \"Johann Haller\") established a [printing press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Printing_press \"Printing press\") in the city[[51]](#cite_note-Haller-53) after [Kasper Straube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kasper_Straube \"Kasper Straube\") had printed the [Calendarium Cracoviense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calendarium_Cracoviense \"Calendarium Cracoviense\"), the first work printed in Poland, in 1473.[[52]](#cite_note-Norman_Davies,_God's_Playground,_vol.1,_chapter_5-54)[[53]](#cite_note-Wieslaw_Wydra_88-55)\n\nIn 1520, the most famous [church bell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_bell \"Church bell\") in Poland, named *[Zygmunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sigismund_Bell \"The Sigismund Bell\")* after [Sigismund I of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_I_of_Poland \"Sigismund I of Poland\"), was cast by Hans Behem.[[54]](#cite_note-dzwon-56) At that time, [Hans Dürer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Dürer \"Hans Dürer\"), a younger brother of artist and thinker [Albrecht Dürer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albrecht_Dürer \"Albrecht Dürer\"), was Sigismund's [court painter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_painter \"Court painter\").[[55]](#cite_note-HansDur-57) [Hans von Kulmbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_von_Kulmbach \"Hans von Kulmbach\") made [altarpieces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altarpiece \"Altarpiece\") for several churches.[[56]](#cite_note-Kulmbach-58) In 1553, the Kazimierz district council gave the Jewish [Qahal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qahal \"Qahal\") (council of a Jewish self-governing community) a licence for the right to build their own interior walls across the western section of the already existing defensive walls. The walls were expanded again in 1608 due to the growth of the community and influx of Jews from Bohemia.[[57]](#cite_note-Kazimierz.com-59) In 1572, King [Sigismund II Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_II_Augustus \"Sigismund II Augustus\"), the last of the Jagiellons, died childless. The Polish throne passed to [Henry III of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_III_of_France \"Henry III of France\") and then to other foreign-based rulers in rapid succession, causing a decline in the city's importance. Furthermore, in 1596, [Sigismund III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_III \"Sigismund III\") of the [House of Vasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Vasa \"House of Vasa\") moved the administrative capital of the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish–Lithuanian_Commonwealth \"Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth\") from Kraków to [Warsaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw \"Warsaw\").[[58]](#cite_note-warsaw-capital-1596-60) The city was destabilised by pillaging in the 1650s during the [Swedish invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deluge_(history) \"Deluge (history)\"), especially during the [1655 siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Kraków \"Siege of Kraków\").[[59]](#cite_note-Milewski2-61) Later in 1707, the city underwent an outbreak of [bubonic plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubonic_plague \"Bubonic plague\") that left 20,000 of the city's residents dead.[[60]](#cite_note-62)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Cracovia_Metropolis_Regni_Poloniae._1618_%28middle_size%29_%2855759065%29.jpg/960px-Cracovia_Metropolis_Regni_Poloniae._1618_%28middle_size%29_%2855759065%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cracovia_Metropolis_Regni_Poloniae._1618_(middle_size)_(55759065).jpg \"View of Kraków (Cracovia) near the end of the 16th century\")\n\nView of Kraków (*Cracovia*) near the end of the 16th century\n\n### 19th century\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=5 \"Edit section: 19th century\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Smuglewicz_Kosciuszko_2.jpg/250px-Smuglewicz_Kosciuszko_2.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Smuglewicz_Kosciuszko_2.jpg)\n\n[Tadeusz Kościuszko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tadeusz_Kościuszko \"Tadeusz Kościuszko\") taking the [oath of loyalty to the Polish nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kościuszko's_proclamation \"Kościuszko's proclamation\") in Kraków's market square (*Rynek*), 1794\n\nAlready weakened during the 18th century, by the mid-1790s the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish–Lithuanian_Commonwealth \"Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth\") had twice been [partitioned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partitions_of_Poland \"Partitions of Poland\") by its neighbors: [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire \"Russian Empire\"), the [Habsburg empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_empire \"Habsburg empire\") and [Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Prussia \"Kingdom of Prussia\").[[61]](#cite_note-The_Polish_struggle_for_freedom-63) In 1791, the Holy Roman Emperor [Leopold II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopold_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor \"Leopold II, Holy Roman Emperor\") changed the status of Kazimierz as a separate city and made it into a district of Kraków. The richer Jewish families began to move out. However, because of the injunction against travel on the [Sabbath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat \"Shabbat\"), most Jewish families stayed relatively close to the historic synagogues. In 1794, [Tadeusz Kościuszko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tadeusz_Kościuszko \"Tadeusz Kościuszko\") initiated an unsuccessful [insurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kościuszko_Uprising \"Kościuszko Uprising\") in [the town's Main Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Square,_Kraków \"Main Square, Kraków\") which, in spite of his victorious [Battle of Racławice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Racławice \"Battle of Racławice\") against a numerically superior [Russian army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Russian_Army \"Imperial Russian Army\"), resulted in the [third and final partition of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Partition_of_Poland \"Third Partition of Poland\"). As a result, Kraków fell under Habsburg rule.[[62]](#cite_note-GR-64)\n\nIn 1802, German became the town's official language. Of the members appointed by the Habsburgs to the municipal council only half were Polish.[[63]](#cite_note-Franaszek-65) From 1796 to 1809, the population of the city rose from 22,000 to 26,000 with an increasing percentage of nobles and officials.[[63]](#cite_note-Franaszek-65) In 1809, [Napoleon Bonaparte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_Bonaparte \"Napoleon Bonaparte\") captured former Polish territories from Austria and made the town part of the [Duchy of Warsaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Warsaw \"Duchy of Warsaw\").[[63]](#cite_note-Franaszek-65) During the time of the Duchy of Warsaw, requirements to upkeep the Polish army followed by tours of Austrian, Polish and Russian troops, plus Russian occupation and a flood in the year 1813 all added up to the adverse development of the city with a high debt burden on public finances and many workshops and trading houses needing to close their activities.[[63]](#cite_note-Franaszek-65)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/J%C3%B3zef_Brodowski_001.JPG/250px-J%C3%B3zef_Brodowski_001.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Józef_Brodowski_001.JPG)\n\nAct of granting the constitution to the [Free City of Cracow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_City_of_Cracow \"Free City of Cracow\"). After the [Partitions of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partitions_of_Poland \"Partitions of Poland\"), Kraków became a city-state and remained the only piece of sovereign Polish territory between 1815 and 1846.\n\nFollowing Napoleon's defeat, the 1815 [Congress of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Vienna \"Congress of Vienna\") restored the pre-war boundaries but also created the partially independent and neutral [Free City of Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_City_of_Kraków \"Free City of Kraków\").[[63]](#cite_note-Franaszek-65) In addition to the historic city of Kraków itself, the Free City included the towns of [Chrzanow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrzanow \"Chrzanow\"), [Trzebinia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trzebinia \"Trzebinia\") and [Nowa Gora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowa_Góra,_Lesser_Poland_Voivodeship \"Nowa Góra, Lesser Poland Voivodeship\") and 224 villages. Outside the city, mining and metallurgy started developing. The population of Kraków itself grew in this time from 23,000 to 43,000; that of the overall republic from 88,000 to 103,000. The population of the city had an increasing number of [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic \"Catholic\") clergy, officials and intelligentsia with which the rich townspeople sympathised. They were opposed to the conservative [landed aristocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landed_aristocracy \"Landed aristocracy\") who also were drawn more and more to the city real estates even though their income still mainly came from their agricultural possessions in the Republic, the Kingdom of Poland and Galicia. The percentage of the Jewish population in the city also increased in this time from 20.8% to 30.4%. However, nationalist sentiment and other political issues led to instability; this culminated in the [Kraków uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_uprising \"Kraków uprising\") of 1846, which was crushed by the Austrian authorities.[[64]](#cite_note-Frommer-66) The Free City was therefore annexed into the Austrian Empire as the [Grand Duchy of Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Kraków \"Grand Duchy of Kraków\") ([Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language \"Polish language\"): *Wielkie Księstwo Krakowskie*, [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language \"German language\"): *Großherzogtum Krakau*), which was legally separate from but administratively part of the [Kingdom of Galicia and Lodomeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Galicia_and_Lodomeria \"Kingdom of Galicia and Lodomeria\") (more simply Austrian Galicia).[[65]](#cite_note-Chambers's_encyclopaedia:_a..._-_Google_Books-67)\n\nDuring the era of the free city, a [free trade zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade_zone \"Free trade zone\") led to positive economic development. But because of the unstable political situation and insecurity about the future, not much of the accumulated wealth was invested.[[63]](#cite_note-Franaszek-65) Through the increase of taxes, customs and regulations, prices soared and the city fell into a recession. From 1844 to 1850 the population was diminished by over 4,000 inhabitants.[[63]](#cite_note-Franaszek-65)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Rz%C4%85ca_Tadeusz%2C_Rynek_G%C5%82%C3%B3wny_w_Krakowie.jpg/250px-Rz%C4%85ca_Tadeusz%2C_Rynek_G%C5%82%C3%B3wny_w_Krakowie.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rząca_Tadeusz,_Rynek_Główny_w_Krakowie.jpg)\n\nFlower vendors in [Rynek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Square,_Kraków \"Main Square, Kraków\")—the first [autochrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autochrome \"Autochrome\") in Poland, dated 1915\n\nIn 1866, Austria granted a degree of autonomy to Galicia after its own defeat in the [Austro-Prussian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austro-Prussian_War \"Austro-Prussian War\").[[66]](#cite_note-(''see:_Franz_Joseph_I_granted_Kraków_the_municipal_government'')-68) Kraków, being politically freer than the Polish cities under Prussian (later German) and Russian rule, became a Polish national symbol and a centre of culture and art, known frequently as the \"Polish Athens\" (*Polskie Ateny*). Many leading Polish artists of the period resided in Kraków,[[67]](#cite_note-google-69) among them the seminal painter [Jan Matejko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Matejko \"Jan Matejko\"),[[68]](#cite_note-Matejko-70) laid to rest at [Rakowicki Cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakowicki_Cemetery \"Rakowicki Cemetery\"), and the founder of modern Polish drama, [Stanisław Wyspiański](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanisław_Wyspiański \"Stanisław Wyspiański\").[[69]](#cite_note-culture-71) [Fin de siècle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fin_de_siècle \"Fin de siècle\") Kraków evolved into a modern metropolis; [running water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tap_water \"Tap water\") and electric [streetcars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streetcars \"Streetcars\") were introduced in 1901, and between 1910 and 1915, Kraków and its surrounding suburban communities were gradually combined into a single administrative unit called Greater Kraków (*Wielki Kraków*).[[70]](#cite_note-Becoming_Metropolitan:_Urban_Selfhood_and_the_Making_of_Modern_Krakow-72)[[71]](#cite_note-Kalendarium-73)\n\nAt the outbreak of [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I \"World War I\") on 3 August 1914, [Józef Piłsudski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Józef_Piłsudski \"Józef Piłsudski\") formed a small [cadre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadre_(military) \"Cadre (military)\") [military unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_organization \"Military organization\"), the [First Cadre Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Cadre_Company \"First Cadre Company\")—the predecessor of the [Polish Legions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Legions_in_World_War_I \"Polish Legions in World War I\")—which set out from Kraków to fight for the liberation of Poland.[[72]](#cite_note-Urb_171-172-74) The city was briefly besieged by Russian troops in November 1914.[[73]](#cite_note-twierdza-75) Austrian rule in Kraków ended in 1918 when the [Polish Liquidation Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Liquidation_Committee \"Polish Liquidation Committee\") assumed power.[[74]](#cite_note-Eastern_Europe:_an_introduction_to..._-_Google_Books-76)[[75]](#cite_note-Encyclopedia_of_Rusyn_history_and..._-_Google_Books-77)\n\n### 20th century to the present\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=6 \"Edit section: 20th century to the present\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Cracow_-_Gumowski_Jan.jpg/250px-Cracow_-_Gumowski_Jan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cracow_-_Gumowski_Jan.jpg)\n\nA vintage [English language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language \"English language\") travel poster promoting tourism to Kraków, 1931\n\nFollowing the emergence of the [Second Polish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Polish_Republic \"Second Polish Republic\") in 1918, Kraków resumed its role as a major Polish academic and cultural centre, with the establishment of new universities such as the [AGH University of Science and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AGH_University_of_Science_and_Technology \"AGH University of Science and Technology\") and the [Jan Matejko Academy of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Matejko_Academy_of_Fine_Arts \"Jan Matejko Academy of Fine Arts\"), as well as several new and essential vocational schools. The city became an important cultural centre for [Polish Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Jews \"Polish Jews\"), including both [Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionist \"Zionist\") and [Bundist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Jewish_Labour_Bund_in_Poland \"General Jewish Labour Bund in Poland\") groups.[[76]](#cite_note-78)[[77]](#cite_note-Krakow_old_scenes,_including_historical_photographs-79)[[78]](#cite_note-80) Kraków was also an influential centre of Jewish spiritual life, with all its manifestations of religious observance—from [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism \"Orthodox Judaism\") to [Hasidic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasidic_Judaism \"Hasidic Judaism\") and [Reform Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism \"Reform Judaism\")—flourishing side by side.[[79]](#cite_note-Sinnreich-81)\n\nFollowing the [invasion of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland \"Invasion of Poland\") by [Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany \"Nazi Germany\") in September 1939, the city of Kraków became part of the [General Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Government \"General Government\"), a separate administrative region of the Third Reich. On 26 October 1939, the Nazi régime set up *[Distrikt Krakau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distrikt_Krakau \"Distrikt Krakau\")*, one of four districts within the General Government. On the same day, the city of Kraków became the capital of the administration.[[80]](#cite_note-82) The General Government was ruled by Governor-General [Hans Frank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Frank \"Hans Frank\"), who was based in the city's Wawel Castle. The Nazis envisioned turning Kraków into a completely Germanised city; after removal of all Jews and Poles, renaming of locations and streets into the German language, and sponsorship of propaganda portraying the city as historically German.[[81]](#cite_note-83) On 28 November 1939, Frank set up *[Judenräte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judenräte \"Judenräte\")* ('Jewish Councils') to be run by Jewish citizens for the purpose of carrying out orders for the Nazis. These orders included the registration of all Jewish people living in each area, the collection of taxes, and the formation of [forced-labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labour_under_German_rule_during_World_War_II \"Forced labour under German rule during World War II\") groups. The Polish [Home Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_Army \"Home Army\") maintained a parallel underground administrative system.[[82]](#cite_note-84)\n\nAt the outbreak of [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II \"World War II\"), some 56,000 Jews resided in Kraków—almost one-quarter of a total population of about 250,000; by November 1939, the Jewish population of the city had grown to approximately 70,000.[[83]](#cite_note-USHMM-Holocaust-Encyclopedia-Krakow-85)[[84]](#cite_note-USHMM-Ghettos-Encyclopedia-VolII-86) According to German statistics from 1940, over 200,000 Jews lived within the entire Kraków District, comprising more than 5 percent of the district's total population. However, these statistics probably underestimate the situation.[[84]](#cite_note-USHMM-Ghettos-Encyclopedia-VolII-86) In November 1939, during an operation known as *[Sonderaktion Krakau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonderaktion_Krakau \"Sonderaktion Krakau\")* ('special operation Kraków'), the Germans arrested more than 180 university professors and academics, and sent them to the [Sachsenhausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sachsenhausen \"Sachsenhausen\") and [Dachau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dachau \"Dachau\") [concentration camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_concentration_camps \"Nazi concentration camps\"), though the survivors were later released on the request of prominent Italians.[[85]](#cite_note-16B._Eastern_Europe_in_World_War_II:_October_1939_–_May_1945.-87)[[86]](#cite_note-sonderaktion-88)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Krakow_Ghetto_Gate_73170.jpg/250px-Krakow_Ghetto_Gate_73170.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Krakow_Ghetto_Gate_73170.jpg)\n\nThe [Kraków Ghetto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Ghetto \"Kraków Ghetto\") was set up in 1941 by the occupational German Nazi [General Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Government \"General Government\").\n\nBefore the formation of [ghettos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghettos \"Ghettos\"), which began in the Kraków District in December 1939, Jews were encouraged to flee the city. For those who remained, the German authorities decided in March 1941 to allocate a then-suburban neighborhood, [Podgórze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podgórze \"Podgórze\"), to become Kraków's ghetto, where many Jews subsequently died of illness or starvation. Initially, most ghettos were open and Jews were allowed to enter and exit freely, but as security became tighter the ghettos were generally closed. From autumn 1941, the [SS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schutzstaffel \"Schutzstaffel\") developed the policy of [extermination through labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extermination_through_labour \"Extermination through labour\"),[[87]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2010171-89) which further worsened the already bleak conditions for Jews. The inhabitants of the [Kraków Ghetto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Ghetto \"Kraków Ghetto\") were later murdered or sent to German [extermination camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extermination_camp \"Extermination camp\"), including [Bełżec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belzec_extermination_camp \"Belzec extermination camp\") and [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp \"Auschwitz concentration camp\"), and to [Kraków-Płaszów concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków-Płaszów_concentration_camp \"Kraków-Płaszów concentration camp\").[[88]](#cite_note-The-Kraków_Ghetto_1940-1943-90) The largest deportations within the Distrikt occurred from June to September 1942. More specifically, mass deportation from Kraków's ghetto occurred in the first week of June 1942,[[84]](#cite_note-USHMM-Ghettos-Encyclopedia-VolII-86) and the ghetto was finally liquidated in March 1943.[[89]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2010376-91)\n\nThe film director [Roman Polanski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Polanski \"Roman Polanski\") survived the Kraków Ghetto. [Oskar Schindler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oskar_Schindler \"Oskar Schindler\") selected employees from the ghetto to work in his enamelware factory *[Deutsche Emailwarenfabrik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Emailwarenfabrik \"Deutsche Emailwarenfabrik\")*, saving them from the camps.[[90]](#cite_note-All_for_Love_-_Google_Books-92)[[91]](#cite_note-Oskar_Schindler:_The_Untold_Account..._-_Google_Books-93) Similarly, many men capable of physical labor were saved from deportation to extermination camps and instead sent to labor camps across the General Government.[[84]](#cite_note-USHMM-Ghettos-Encyclopedia-VolII-86) By September 1943, the last of the Jews from the Kraków Ghetto had been deported. Although [looted by occupational authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Looting_of_Poland_in_World_War_II \"Looting of Poland in World War II\"), Kraków remained relatively undamaged at the end of World War II,[[92]](#cite_note-LukZaw-94) with most of the city's historical and architectural legacy spared. Soviet forces under the command of Marshal [Ivan Konev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Konev \"Ivan Konev\") entered the city on 18 January 1945 and began arresting Poles loyal to the [Polish government-in-exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_government-in-exile \"Polish government-in-exile\") or those who had served in the Home Army.[[93]](#cite_note-95)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ad/Rozw%C3%B3j_terytorialny_Krakowa_-_EN.svg/500px-Rozw%C3%B3j_terytorialny_Krakowa_-_EN.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rozwój_terytorialny_Krakowa_-_EN.svg)\n\nKraków's territorial growth from the late 18th to the 20th century\n\nAfter the war, under the [Polish People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_People's_Republic \"Polish People's Republic\") (officially declared in 1952), the intellectual and academic community of Kraków came under complete political control. The universities were soon deprived of their printing rights and autonomy.[[94]](#cite_note-autonomy-96) The [Stalinist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalinist \"Stalinist\") government of Poland ordered the construction of the country's largest [steel mill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_mill \"Steel mill\") in the newly created suburb of [Nowa Huta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowa_Huta \"Nowa Huta\").[[95]](#cite_note-krakow_history-97) The creation of the giant Lenin Steelworks (now [Sendzimir Steelworks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tadeusz_Sendzimir_Steelworks \"Tadeusz Sendzimir Steelworks\") owned by [Mittal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mittal_Steel_Company \"Mittal Steel Company\")) sealed Kraków's transformation from a university city into an industrial centre.[[96]](#cite_note-communist_era-98)\n\nIn an effort that spanned two decades, [Karol Wojtyła](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karol_Wojtyła \"Karol Wojtyła\"), the cardinal archbishop of Kraków from 1964 to 1978, successfully lobbied for permission to build the first churches in the newly industrialized suburbs.[[96]](#cite_note-communist_era-98)[[97]](#cite_note-NH-anthology-99) In 1978, the Catholic Church elevated Wojtyła to the [papacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papacy \"Papacy\") as [John Paul II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Paul_II \"John Paul II\"), the first non-Italian pope in over 450 years. In the same year, [UNESCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO \"UNESCO\"), following the application of local authorities, placed Kraków Old Town on the first list of [World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Site \"World Heritage Site\").[[98]](#cite_note-Woodward-100)\n\n## Geography\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=7 \"Edit section: Geography\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/20200529_Widok_ze_Ska%C5%82y_Okr%C4%85%C5%BCek_na_Opactwo_w_Ty%C5%84cu_1735_2128.jpg/250px-20200529_Widok_ze_Ska%C5%82y_Okr%C4%85%C5%BCek_na_Opactwo_w_Ty%C5%84cu_1735_2128.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:20200529_Widok_ze_Skały_Okrążek_na_Opactwo_w_Tyńcu_1735_2128.jpg)\n\n[Tyniec Abbey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyniec \"Tyniec\") with the [Bielany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bielany,_Kraków \"Bielany, Kraków\") [Camaldolese Hermit Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camaldolese_Hermit_Monastery,_Kraków \"Camaldolese Hermit Monastery, Kraków\") in the distance\n\nKraków lies in the southern part of Poland, on the [Vistula River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vistula_River \"Vistula River\"), approximately 219 m (719 ft) [above sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Above_sea_level \"Above sea level\").[[99]](#cite_note-SiT-101) The city is located on the border between different [physiographic regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physiographic_region \"Physiographic region\"): the [Kraków-Częstochowa Upland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków-Częstochowa_Upland \"Kraków-Częstochowa Upland\") in the north-western parts of the city, the [Małopolska Upland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Małopolska_Upland \"Małopolska Upland\") in the north-east, the [Sandomierz Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandomierz_Basin \"Sandomierz Basin\") (east) and the [Western Beskidian Foothills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Beskidian_Foothills \"Western Beskidian Foothills\") of the [Carpathians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathians \"Carpathians\") (south).[[100]](#cite_note-102)\n\nThere are five [nature reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_reserve \"Nature reserve\") in Kraków, with a combined area of ca. 48.6 hectares (120 acres).[[101]](#cite_note-ZZM-103) Due to their ecological value, these areas are legally protected.[[101]](#cite_note-ZZM-103) The western part of the city, along its northern and north-western side, borders an area of international significance known as the Jurassic [Bielany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bielany,_Kraków \"Bielany, Kraków\")-[Tyniec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyniec \"Tyniec\") refuge.[[101]](#cite_note-ZZM-103) The main motives for the protection of this area include plant and animal wildlife and the area's [geomorphological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geomorphological \"Geomorphological\") features and landscape.[[102]](#cite_note-Pattern_of_karst_landscape_of_the_Cracow_Upland_(South_Poland)-104) Another part of the city is located within the ecological 'corridor' of the Vistula River valley. This corridor is also assessed as being of international significance as part of the Pan-European ecological network.[[103]](#cite_note-The_forms_of_nature_protection_within_the_city_limits-105)\n\n### Climate\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=8 \"Edit section: Climate\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/20180506_Ko%C5%9Bci%C3%B3%C5%82_%C5%9Bw._Augustyna_i_%C5%9Bw._Jana_Chrzciciela_w_Krakowie_2989_DxO.jpg/250px-20180506_Ko%C5%9Bci%C3%B3%C5%82_%C5%9Bw._Augustyna_i_%C5%9Bw._Jana_Chrzciciela_w_Krakowie_2989_DxO.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:20180506_Kościół_św._Augustyna_i_św._Jana_Chrzciciela_w_Krakowie_2989_DxO.jpg)\n\nConvent of Norbertine Sisters in Kraków-Zwierzyniec and the [Vistula River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vistula_River \"Vistula River\") during the summer season\n\nKraków has a [humid continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate \"Humid continental climate\"), denoted by [Köppen classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Köppen_classification \"Köppen classification\") as *Dfb*, somewhat bordering on an [oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate \"Oceanic climate\") (*Cfb*); with climate change winters are rapidly becoming milder and summers longer and hotter more like humid subtropical climate (Cfa), hot summers days above 30C are increasingly common,[[104]](#cite_note-106) but with winter temperatures on average still below freezing, it is perhaps best defined as having a semicontinental climate.[[105]](#cite_note-Warsaw-107)[[106]](#cite_note-108) In older reference periods it was classified as a [warm summer continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warm_summer_continental_climate \"Warm summer continental climate\") (*Dfb*).[[107]](#cite_note-109)[[108]](#cite_note-110) By classification of [Wincenty Okołowicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wincenty_Okołowicz \"Wincenty Okołowicz\"), it has a [warm temperate climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_climate \"Temperate climate\") in the centre of [continental Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe \"Continental Europe\") with the \"fusion\" of different features.[[109]](#cite_note-111)\n\nDue to its geographic location, the city may be under marine influence, sometimes [Arctic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic \"Arctic\") influence, but without direct influence, giving the city variable meteorological conditions over short spaces of time.[[110]](#cite_note-112)[[111]](#cite_note-113) The city lies in proximity to the [Tatra Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatra_Mountains \"Tatra Mountains\") and there are often occurrences of a [foehn wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foehn_wind \"Foehn wind\") called [halny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halny \"Halny\"), causing temperatures to rise rapidly.[[112]](#cite_note-SEDNO-114) In relation to Warsaw, temperatures are very similar for most of the year, except that in the colder months [southern Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Poland \"Regions of Poland\") has a larger daily temperature range, more moderate winds, generally more rainy days and with greater chances of clear skies on average, especially in winter. The higher sun angle also allows for a longer [growing season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growing_season \"Growing season\").[[113]](#cite_note-115) In addition, for older data there was less sun than the capital of the country, about 30 minutes daily per year, but both have small differences in relative humidity and the direction of the winds is northeast.[[105]](#cite_note-Warsaw-107)\n\nThe climate table below presents weather data with averages from 1991 to 2020, sunshine ranges from 1971 to 2000, and valid extremes from 1951 to the present day:\n\n| Climate data for Kraków-Airport
