Cityscape\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=9 \"Edit section: Cityscape\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Krakow_-_Cloth_Hall_from_Basilica_-_1.jpg/250px-Krakow_-_Cloth_Hall_from_Basilica_-_1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Krakow_-_Cloth_Hall_from_Basilica_-_1.jpg)\n\nThe [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_in_Poland \"Renaissance in Poland\") Cloth Hall (*[Sukiennice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukiennice \"Sukiennice\")*) in [Main Market Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Market_Square,_Kraków \"Main Market Square, Kraków\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/DJI_0352_Wawel.jpg/250px-DJI_0352_Wawel.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:DJI_0352_Wawel.jpg)\n\n[Wawel Royal Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Castle \"Wawel Castle\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Barbakan_Krakow_z_ulicy_Basztowej.jpg/250px-Barbakan_Krakow_z_ulicy_Basztowej.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Barbakan_Krakow_z_ulicy_Basztowej.jpg)\n\nThe [Kraków Barbican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Barbican \"Kraków Barbican\"), dating from around 1498, was once a fortified outpost of the inner medieval city.\n\nKraków provides a showcase setting for many historic forms of [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture \"Architecture\") developed over the ten centuries, especially [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture \"Gothic architecture\"), [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_architecture \"Renaissance architecture\") and [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_architecture \"Baroque architecture\") styles.[[127]](#cite_note-Hourihane-129) Renowned artisans and skilled craftsmen from present-day [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italy \"Italy\") and [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire \"Holy Roman Empire\") were brought and sponsored by kings or nobles who contributed to [architectural wealth and diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Poland \"Architecture of Poland\").[[127]](#cite_note-Hourihane-129) The [Brick Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brick_Gothic \"Brick Gothic\") manner as well as countless structural elements such as the Renaissance [attics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attic_(architecture) \"Attic (architecture)\") with decorative [pinnacles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinnacle \"Pinnacle\") became recognisable features of historical buildings in Kraków.[[25]](#cite_note-Małecki-27) Built from its earliest nucleus outward, the city's monuments can be seen in historical order by walking from the city centre out, towards its newer districts.[[127]](#cite_note-Hourihane-129)\n\nKraków's historic centre, which includes [the Old Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Old_Town \"Kraków Old Town\") (*Stare Miasto*), the [Main Market Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Market_Square,_Kraków \"Main Market Square, Kraków\") (*Rynek Główny*), the [Cloth Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Cloth_Hall \"Kraków Cloth Hall\") (*Sukiennice*), the [Barbican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbican \"Barbican\") (*Barbakan*), [St. Florian's Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Florian's_Gate \"St. Florian's Gate\"), [Kazimierz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazimierz \"Kazimierz\") and the [Wawel Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Castle \"Wawel Castle\"), was included as the first of its kind on the list of [UNESCO World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_World_Heritage_Sites \"UNESCO World Heritage Sites\") in 1978.[[17]](#cite_note-Centre-19) The central core surrounded by [Planty Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planty_Park \"Planty Park\") remains the most prominent example of an old town in the country, with the medieval street layout still in existence.[[128]](#cite_note-Gustafson-130) Kraków was the royal capital of Poland for many centuries, until [Sigismund III Vasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_III_Vasa \"Sigismund III Vasa\") relocated the court to [Warsaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw \"Warsaw\") in 1596.[[129]](#cite_note-Wilson-131) The district is bisected by [the Royal Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Road,_Kraków \"Royal Road, Kraków\"), the coronation route traversed by the [Kings of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kings_of_Poland \"Kings of Poland\"). Several important monuments were lost in the course of history, notably the [Ratusz town hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Town_Hall \"Kraków Town Hall\").[[130]](#cite_note-132) However, the Gothic [Town Hall Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Town_Hall_Tower,_Kraków \"Town Hall Tower, Kraków\") measuring 70 m (229 ft 8 in) in height remains standing.[[24]](#cite_note-Nungovitch1-26)\n\nIn addition to the old town, the city's district of [Kazimierz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazimierz \"Kazimierz\") is particularly notable for its many renaissance buildings and picturesque streets, as well as the historic Jewish quarter located in the north-eastern part of Kazimierz. Kazimierz was founded in the 14th century to the south-east of the city centre and soon became a wealthy, well-populated area where construction of imposing properties became commonplace. Perhaps the most important feature of medieval Kazimierz was the only major, permanent bridge (*Pons Regalis*) across the northern arm of the Vistula. This natural barrier used to separate Kazimierz from the Old Town for several centuries, while the bridge connected Kraków to the [Wieliczka Salt Mine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wieliczka_Salt_Mine \"Wieliczka Salt Mine\") and the lucrative Hungarian trade route. The last structure at this location (at the end of modern Stradom Street) was dismantled in 1880 when the northern arm of the river was filled in with earth and rock, and subsequently built over.[[42]](#cite_note-Świszczowski-44)[[131]](#cite_note-wandaluzja-1-133)\n\nBy the 1930s, Kraków had 120 officially [registered synagogues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synagogues_of_Kraków \"Synagogues of Kraków\") and prayer houses that spanned across the old city. Much of Jewish intellectual life had moved to new centres like [Podgórze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podgórze \"Podgórze\").[[132]](#cite_note-krakow.jewish.org.pl-pdf-134) This, in turn, led to the redevelopment and renovation of much of Kazimierz and the development of new districts in Kraków. Most historic buildings in central Kazimierz today are preserved in their original form. Some old buildings, however, were not repaired after the devastation brought by the Second World War, and have remained empty. Most recent efforts at restoring the historic neighborhoods gained new impetus around 1993. Kazimierz is now a well-visited area, seeing a booming growth in Jewish-themed restaurants, bars, bookstores and souvenir shops.[[133]](#cite_note-135)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/%C3%89glise_et_couvent_des_Paulins_%C3%A0_Skalka%2C_de_plus_loin.jpg/250px-%C3%89glise_et_couvent_des_Paulins_%C3%A0_Skalka%2C_de_plus_loin.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Église_et_couvent_des_Paulins_à_Skalka,_de_plus_loin.jpg)\n\n[Skałka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skałka \"Skałka\") (\"Small Rock\") Church, and the adjacent monastery of [Pauline Fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauline_Fathers \"Pauline Fathers\"), is a place of burial for distinguished Poles and Cracovians.\n\nAs the city of Kraków began to expand further under the rule of the [Austro-Hungarian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austro-Hungarian_Empire \"Austro-Hungarian Empire\"), the new architectural styles also developed. Key buildings from the 19th and early 20th centuries in Kraków include the Jan Matejko Academy of Fine Arts, the directorate of the Polish State Railways as well as the original complex of [Kraków Główny railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Główny_railway_station \"Kraków Główny railway station\") and the city's [Academy of Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_University_of_Economics \"Kraków University of Economics\"). It was also at around that time that Kraków's first radial boulevards began to appear, with the city undergoing a large-scale program aimed at transforming the ancient Polish capital into a sophisticated regional centre of the Austro-Hungarian Empire. New representative government buildings and multi-story tenement houses were built at around that time. Much of the urban-planning beyond the walls of the Old Town was done by Polish architects and engineers trained in [Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vienna \"Vienna\"). Some major projects of the era include the development of the [Jagiellonian University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagiellonian_University \"Jagiellonian University\")'s new premises and the building of the [Collegium Novum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collegium_Novum \"Collegium Novum\") just west of the Old Town. The imperial style planning of the city's further development continued until the return of Poland's independence, following the First World War. Early modernist style in Kraków is represented by such masterpieces as [the Palace of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Society_of_Friends_of_Fine_Arts \"Kraków Society of Friends of Fine Arts\") by [Franciszek Mączyński](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franciszek_Mączyński \"Franciszek Mączyński\") and the 'House under the Globe'. [Secession style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vienna_Secession#Architecture \"Vienna Secession\") architecture, which had arrived in Kraków from [Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vienna \"Vienna\"), became popular towards the end of [the Partitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partitions_of_Poland \"Partitions of Poland\").[[134]](#cite_note-Strasz-136)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Palace_of_Art%2C_4%2CSzczepanski_square%2Csecession_building%2C_Krakow_Old_Town.jpg/250px-Palace_of_Art%2C_4%2CSzczepanski_square%2Csecession_building%2C_Krakow_Old_Town.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Palace_of_Art,_4,Szczepanski_square,secession_building,_Krakow_Old_Town.jpg)\n\n[Palace of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Society_of_Friends_of_Fine_Arts \"Kraków Society of Friends of Fine Arts\") at Szczepański Square is an example of [Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau \"Art Nouveau\") architecture in central Kraków.[[135]](#cite_note-Palace_of_Art-137)\n\nWith Poland's regained independence came the major change in the fortunes of Kraków—now the second most important city of a sovereign nation. The state began to make new plans for the city development and commissioned a number of representative buildings. The predominant style for new projects was modernism with various interpretations of the art-deco style.[[136]](#cite_note-Vogt-Nassery-138) Important buildings constructed in the style of [Polish modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_modernism \"Polish modernism\") include the Feniks 'LOT' building on Basztowa Street, the Feniks department store on the [Main Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Square,_Kraków \"Main Square, Kraków\") and the Municipal Savings Bank on Szczepański Square. The Józef Piłsudski house is also of note as a particularly good example of interwar architecture in the city.[[137]](#cite_note-Kraków,_Oleandry_–_Dom_im._Józefa_Piłsudskiego-139)\n\nAfter the Second World War, new Communist government adopted [Stalinist monumentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalinist_architecture \"Stalinist architecture\"). The doctrine of [Socialist realism in Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_realism_in_Poland \"Socialist realism in Poland\"), as in other countries of the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc \"Eastern Bloc\"), was enforced from 1949 to 1956. It involved all domains of art, but its most spectacular achievements were made in the field of urban design. The guidelines for this new trend were spelled-out in a 1949 resolution of the National Council of Party Architects. Architecture was to become a weapon in establishing the new social order by the communists.[[138]](#cite_note-inyourpocket/sightseeing-140) The ideological impact of urban design was valued more than aesthetics. It aimed at expressing persistence and power. This form of architecture was implemented in the new industrial district of Nowa Huta with apartment blocks constructed according to a Stalinist blueprint, with repetitious courtyards and wide, tree-lined avenues.[[139]](#cite_note-Nh.pl-1-141)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Poranna_cisza_Alei_R%C3%B3%C5%BC.jpg/250px-Poranna_cisza_Alei_R%C3%B3%C5%BC.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Poranna_cisza_Alei_Róż.jpg)\n\n[Nowa Huta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowa_Huta \"Nowa Huta\") Avenue of Roses\n\nSince the style of the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_in_Poland \"Renaissance in Poland\") was generally regarded as the most revered in old Polish architecture, it was also used for augmenting Poland's [Socialist national](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_realism_in_Poland \"Socialist realism in Poland\") format. However, in the course of incorporating the principles of Socialist realism, there were quite a few deviations introduced by the communists. From 1953, critical opinions in the Party were increasingly frequent, and the doctrine was given up in 1956 marking the end of [Stalinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalinism_in_Poland \"Stalinism in Poland\").[[140]](#cite_note-SOCREALIZM_in_Poland_(1949–1955)-142) The [soc-realist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socrealism \"Socrealism\") centre of Nowa Huta is considered to be a meritorious monument of the times. This period in postwar architecture was followed by the mass-construction of large [Panel System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plattenbau \"Plattenbau\") apartment blocks, most of which were built outside the city centre and thus do not encroach upon the beauty of the old or new towns. Some examples of the new style (e.g., Hotel Cracovia) recently listed as heritage monuments were built during the latter half of the 20th century in Kraków.[[141]](#cite_note-naszemiasto-cracovia-143)\n\nAfter the [Revolutions of 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1989 \"Revolutions of 1989\") and the birth of the [Third Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Polish_Republic \"Third Polish Republic\") in the latter half of the 20th century,[[142]](#cite_note-144) a number of new architectural projects were completed, including the construction of large business parks and commercial facilities such as the [Galeria Krakowska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galeria_Krakowska \"Galeria Krakowska\"), or infrastructure investments like the [Kraków Fast Tram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Fast_Tram \"Kraków Fast Tram\"). A good example of this would be the [Manggha Museum of Japanese Art and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manggha_Museum_of_Japanese_Art_and_Technology \"Manggha Museum of Japanese Art and Technology\") designed by [Arata Isozaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arata_Isozaki \"Arata Isozaki\"), the 2007-built *Pawilon Wyspiański 2000*,[[143]](#cite_note-sztuka-architektury1-145) which is used as a multi-purpose information and exhibition space, or the Małopolski Garden of Arts (*[Małopolski Ogród Sztuki](https://pl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Małopolski%20Ogród%20Sztuki%20w%20Krakowie \"pl:Małopolski Ogród Sztuki w Krakowie\")*), a multi-purpose exhibition and theatre complex located in the historic Old Town.[[144]](#cite_note-146)\n\nFurther information: [Main Post Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Post_Office,_Kraków \"Main Post Office, Kraków\") and [Stained Glass Museum, Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stained_Glass_Museum,_Kraków \"Stained Glass Museum, Kraków\")\n\n### Parks and gardens\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=10 \"Edit section: Parks and gardens\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Planty_Park%2C_autumn%2C_Old_Town%2C_Krakow%2C_Poland.jpg/250px-Planty_Park%2C_autumn%2C_Old_Town%2C_Krakow%2C_Poland.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Planty_Park,_autumn,_Old_Town,_Krakow,_Poland.jpg)\n\n[Planty Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planty_Park \"Planty Park\") surrounds Kraków's Old Town.\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Planty_Garden%2C_pavilion%2C_Old_Town%2C_Krakow%2C_Poland.JPG/250px-Planty_Garden%2C_pavilion%2C_Old_Town%2C_Krakow%2C_Poland.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Planty_Garden,_pavilion,_Old_Town,_Krakow,_Poland.JPG)\n\nA pavilion within the Planty Park during winter\n\nThere are about 40 parks in Kraków, including dozens of gardens and forests.[[145]](#cite_note-wse.krakow-147) Several, like the [Planty Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planty_Park \"Planty Park\"), [Botanical Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botanic_Garden_of_the_Jagiellonian_University \"Botanic Garden of the Jagiellonian University\"), [Zoological Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Zoo \"Kraków Zoo\"), [Royal Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Garden_in_Kraków \"Royal Garden in Kraków\"), [Park Krakowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Krakowski \"Park Krakowski\"), [Jordan Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_Park \"Jordan Park\") and [Błonia Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Błonia_Park \"Błonia Park\") are located in the centre of the city; with others, such as Zakrzówek, [Wanda Green Ravine Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wanda_Green_Ravine_Park \"Wanda Green Ravine Park\"), Wolski forest, Strzelecki Park and Lotników Park in the surrounding districts.[[145]](#cite_note-wse.krakow-147) Parks cover about 318.5 hectares (787 acres; 1.23 mi2) of the city.[[101]](#cite_note-ZZM-103)\n\nThe best-known park in Kraków is the Planty Park. Established between 1822 and 1830 in place of the old city walls, it forms a [green belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_belt \"Green belt\") around the Old Town and consists of a chain of smaller gardens designed in various styles and adorned with monuments. The park has an area of 21 hectares (52 acres) and a length of 4 kilometres (2.5 mi), forming a scenic walkway popular with Cracovians.[[146]](#cite_note-Strzala1-148)\n\nJordan Park, founded in 1889 by [Henryk Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henryk_Jordan \"Henryk Jordan\"), was the first public park of its kind in Europe.[[147]](#cite_note-grodzka.net-149) Built on the banks of the [Rudawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudawa_(river) \"Rudawa (river)\"), the park was equipped with running and exercise tracks, playgrounds, a swimming pool, [amphitheatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphitheatre \"Amphitheatre\"), pavilions, and a pond for [boat rowing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rowing \"Rowing\") and [water bicycles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_bicycle \"Water bicycle\"). It is located in the grounds of one of the city's larger parks, Błonia Park.[[148]](#cite_note-History,_philosophy_and_photographs-150) The less prominent Park Krakowski, founded in 1885 by [Stanisław Rehman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanisław_Rehman \"Stanisław Rehman\"), was a popular destination point for Cracovians at the end of the 19th century, but has since been greatly reduced in size because of rapid [real estate development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_estate_development \"Real estate development\").[[149]](#cite_note-encyklopedia3-151)\n\n### Environment\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=11 \"Edit section: Environment\")]\n\nThere are five [nature reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_Poland \"Protected areas of Poland\") in Kraków with a total area of 48.6 hectares (120 acres).[[150]](#cite_note-gdos-152) Smaller green zones constitute parts of the [Kraków-Częstochowa Upland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków-Częstochowa_Upland \"Kraków-Częstochowa Upland\") Jurassic Landscape Parks' Board, which deals with the protection areas of the Polish Jura. Under its jurisdiction are: the [Bielany-Tyniec Landscape Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bielany-Tyniec_Landscape_Park \"Bielany-Tyniec Landscape Park\") (Park Bielańsko-Tyniecki), [Tenczynek Landscape Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenczynek_Landscape_Park \"Tenczynek Landscape Park\") (Park Tenczyński) and [Kraków Valleys Landscape Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Valleys_Landscape_Park \"Kraków Valleys Landscape Park\") (Park Krajobrazowy Dolinki Krakowskie), with their watersheds.[[101]](#cite_note-ZZM-103) The natural reserves of the Polish Jura Chain are part of the [CORINE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CORINE \"CORINE\") biotopes programme due to their unique flora, fauna, geomorphology and landscape.[[151]](#cite_note-Parkes-153) The western part of Kraków constitutes the so-called Obszar Krakowski ecological network, including the ecological corridor of the Vistula. The southern slopes of limestone hills provide conditions for the development of [thermophilous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermophilous \"Thermophilous\") vegetation, grasslands and [shrubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrubland \"Shrubland\").[[151]](#cite_note-Parkes-153)[[152]](#cite_note-MAK-154)\n\nThe city is spaced along an extended latitudinal transect of the [Vistula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vistula \"Vistula\") River Valley with a network of tributaries including its right tributary [Wilga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilga_(Krakow) \"Wilga (Krakow)\"), and left: [Rudawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudawa_(river) \"Rudawa (river)\"), Białucha, [Dłubnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dłubnia \"Dłubnia\") and Sanka.[[153]](#cite_note-Burek-155) The rivers and their valleys along with bodies of water are considered some of the most interesting natural sights of Kraków.[[153]](#cite_note-Burek-155)\n\nKraków and its mountainous environs often suffer from Europe's worst [air pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_pollution \"Air pollution\") because of [smog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smog \"Smog\"), caused by burning [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal \"Coal\") for heating, especially in winter.[[154]](#cite_note-156)\n\n## Governance\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=12 \"Edit section: Governance\")]\n\nFurther information: [Local government in Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_Kraków \"Local government in Kraków\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Podgorze_City_Hall_%28new%29%2C_Podgorze%2CKrakow%2CPoland.JPG/250px-Podgorze_City_Hall_%28new%29%2C_Podgorze%2CKrakow%2CPoland.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Podgorze_City_Hall_(new),_Podgorze,Krakow,Poland.JPG)\n\nThe New Town Hall of [Podgórze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podgórze \"Podgórze\"), which used to be a self-governing independent town until its incorporation into Kraków in 1915\n\nThe [Kraków City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_City_Council \"Kraków City Council\") has 43 elected members,[[155]](#cite_note-Radni_Miasta_Krakowa_V_kadencji_(''Kraków_City_Councillors_of_the_5th_term'')-157) one of whom is the [mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_the_Royal_City_of_Kraków \"Mayor of the Royal City of Kraków\"), or President of Kraków, elected every four years. The election of the City Council and of the local [head of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_government \"Head of government\"),[[156]](#cite_note-BPI_113-158) which takes place at the same time, is based on legislation introduced on 20 June 2002. The President of Kraków, re-elected for his fourth term in 2014, is [Jacek Majchrowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacek_Majchrowski \"Jacek Majchrowski\").[[157]](#cite_note-Municipality,_Mayor-159) Several members of the Polish national Parliament ([Sejm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sejm \"Sejm\")) are elected from the [Kraków constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Members_of_Polish_Sejm_elected_from_Kraków_constituency \"Members of Polish Sejm elected from Kraków constituency\").[[158]](#cite_note-Members_of_Polish_Sejm_elected_from_Kraków_constituency_-_VisWiki-160) The [city's official symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbols_of_Kraków \"Symbols of Kraków\") include a [coat of arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms \"Coat of arms\"), a flag, a seal, and a banner.[[159]](#cite_note-ordinance-161)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Krak%C3%B3w_civic_centre_%28Wielopolski_Palace%29%2C_3-4_Wszystkich_%C5%9Awi%C4%99tych_%28All_Saints%29_square%2C_Old_Town%2C_Krak%C3%B3w%2C_Poland.jpg/250px-Krak%C3%B3w_civic_centre_%28Wielopolski_Palace%29%2C_3-4_Wszystkich_%C5%9Awi%C4%99tych_%28All_Saints%29_square%2C_Old_Town%2C_Krak%C3%B3w%2C_Poland.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kraków_civic_centre_(Wielopolski_Palace),_3-4_Wszystkich_Świętych_(All_Saints)_square,_Old_Town,_Kraków,_Poland.jpg)\n\n[Wielopolski Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wielopolski_Palace \"Wielopolski Palace\") from 1560, the seat of Kraków's mayor, administration and city council\n\nResponsibilities of Kraków's president include drafting and implementing resolutions, enacting city bylaws, managing the city budget, employing city administrators, and preparing against floods and [natural disasters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_disaster \"Natural disaster\").[[156]](#cite_note-BPI_113-158) The president fulfills his duties with the help of the City Council, city managers and city inspectors. In the 1990s, the [city government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_government \"City government\") was reorganised to better differentiate between its [political agenda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_agenda \"Political agenda\") and administrative functions. As a result, the Office of Public Information was created to handle inquiries and foster communication between city departments and citizens at large.[[160]](#cite_note-Institutional_Capital:_Building_Post-communist_Government_Performance-162)\n\nIn 2000, the city government introduced a new long-term program called \"Safer City\" in cooperation with the Police, Traffic, Social Services, Fire, [Public Safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Safety \"Public Safety\"), and the Youth Departments. Subsequently, the number of criminal offences dropped by 3 percent between 2000 and 2001, and the rate of detection increased by 1.4 percent to a total of 30.2 percent in the same period.[[161]](#cite_note-krakow-163) The city is receiving help in carrying out the program from all educational institutions and the local media, including TV, radio and the press.\n\n### Districts\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Krak%C3%B3w&action=edit&section=13 \"Edit section: Districts\")]\n\nMain article: [Districts of Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Kraków \"Districts of Kraków\")\n\nKraków is divided into 18 administrative districts (*[dzielnica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzielnica \"Dzielnica\")*) or boroughs, each with a degree of autonomy within its own municipal government.[[162]](#cite_note-districts-164) Prior to March 1991, the city had been divided into four quarters which still give a sense of identity to Kraków: the towns of [Podgórze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podgórze \"Podgórze\"), [Nowa Huta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowa_Huta \"Nowa Huta\") and [Krowodrza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krowodrza \"Krowodrza\"), which were amalgamated into the city as it expanded; and the ancient town centre of Kraków itself.[[162]](#cite_note-districts-164)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Kanonicza_street%2C_view_from_S%2C_Old_Town%2C_Krak%C3%B3w%2C_Poland.jpg/250px-Kanonicza_street%2C_view_from_S%2C_Old_Town%2C_Krak%C3%B3w%2C_Poland.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kanonicza_street,_view_from_S,_Old_Town,_Kraków,_Poland.jpg)\n\n[Kanonicza Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanonicza_Street,_Kraków \"Kanonicza Street, Kraków\") in the Old Town (*[Stare Miasto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Old_Town \"Kraków Old Town\")*) district\n\nThe oldest neighborhoods of Kraków were incorporated into the city before the late 18th century. These include the Old Town (*[Stare Miasto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Old_Town \"Kraków Old Town\")*), once contained within the [city defensive walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florian_Gate \"Florian Gate\") and now encircled by the Planty park; the [Wawel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel \"Wawel\") District, which is the site of the Royal Castle and the cathedral; Stradom and [Kazimierz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazimierz \"Kazimierz\") with its historic Jewish quarter, the latter originally divided into Christian and Jewish quarters;[[163]](#cite_note-Krakow,_Auschwitz_and_Warsaw-165) and the ancient town of [Kleparz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kleparz \"Kleparz\").\n\nMajor districts added in the 19th and 20th centuries include [Podgórze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podgórze \"Podgórze\")—until 1915, a separate town on the southern bank of the Vistula—and Nowa Huta, to the east of the city centre, which was built after World War II.\n\nAmong the most notable historic districts of the city are: Wawel Hill, home to [Wawel Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Castle \"Wawel Castle\") and [Wawel Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wawel_Cathedral \"Wawel Cathedral\"), where many historic Polish kings are buried; the medieval Old Town, with its 200-metre-square (660 ft) [Main Market Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Market_Square,_Kraków \"Main Market Square, Kraków\"); dozens of old churches and museums; the 14th-century buildings of the Jagiellonian University; and Kazimierz, the historical centre of Kraków's Jewish social and religious life.[[164]](#cite_note-Krakow-166)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Ko%C5%9Bci%C3%B3%C5%82_%C5%9Bw._J%C3%B3zefa_Krak%C3%B3w.jpg/250px-Ko%C5%9Bci%C3%B3%C5%82_%C5%9Bw._J%C3%B3zefa_Krak%C3%B3w.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kościół_św._Józefa_Kraków.jpg)\n\nRynek Podgórski - main square of [Podgórze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podgórze \"Podgórze\") district with [St. Joseph's Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Joseph's_Church,_Podgórze \"St. Joseph's Church, Podgórze\")\n\nThe Old Town district of Kraków is home to about six thousand historic sites and more than two million works of art.[[165]](#cite_note-architecture72-167) Its rich variety of heritage architecture includes Romanesque (e.g. [St. Andrew's Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Andrew's_Church,_Kraków \"St. Andrew's Church, Kraków\")), Renaissance (e.g. [Kraków Cloth Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraków_Cloth_Hall \"Kraków Cloth Hall\")), Baroque (e.g. [Saints Peter and Paul Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saints_Peter_and_Paul_Church,_Kraków \"Saints Peter and Paul Church, Kraków\")) and Gothic buildings. Kraków's palaces, churches, theatres and mansions display a great variety of colour, architectural details, [stained glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stained_glass \"Stained glass\"), paintings, sculptures, and furnishings.[[166]](#cite_note-168)\n\nIn the Market Square stands the Gothic [St. Mary's Basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_Assumption_of_the_Blessed_Virgin_Mary,_Kraków \"Church of the Assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary, Kraków\") (*Kościół Mariacki*). Rebuilt in the 14th century, it features the famous wooden altar ([Altarpiece of Veit Stoss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altarpiece_of_Veit_Stoss \"Altarpiece of Veit Stoss\")), the largest Gothic altarpiece in the world,[[167]](#cite_note-Kurtz-169) carved by [Veit Stoss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veit_Stoss \"Veit Stoss\").\n\n| District | Population | Area \"Nowa Huta\") (XVIII) | 58,320 | 6,552.52 ha (65.5252 km2) |\n| Total | 760,700 | 32,680.00 ha (326.8000 km2) |\n\nThe current divisions were introduced by the Kraków City Hall on 19 April 1995. The districts were each assigned a [Roman numeral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_numeral \"Roman numeral\") as well as a name:[[169]](#cite_note-krakow1-171) [Stare
